# Work log — 2026-08

Running notes on uncommitted work, newest first. Each entry is roughly what a commit message would
have said. Past months live beside this file.

## 2026-08-21 - The substitution rule, and a nine-day contradiction the owner settled

U8.7, plus the half of U8.11 that survived it. Gate 9/9, **4,422** per version (from 4,405).

### ⛔⛔ Two design pages had contradicted each other for nine days

STATE.md's Fasteners row (2026-07-30, *reaffirmed 2026-08-15*) said the benches have **"no dies, no
bolts"**. `machining-line.md` § Tooling said *"the heading, nail and rivet benches take dies"* - and
`ItemDie` had shipped in exlib on that reading. I built the benches die-fed off the mechanics page and only
found the decision log's opposite ruling while reading U8.7.

★★ **The owner kept the dies, with a reason neither page carried: dies are wanted for the steam hammer's
stamping too.** So the contract has a second consumer and is not the nail bench's alone. What the original
ruling actually rejected - the die-fed **bolt** route, and a heading bench standing in for two machines -
stays rejected. STATE.md now records the amendment rather than leaving the two pages to disagree again.

⛔ **The lesson is not "trust the newer page".** Both pages were confident and neither cited the other. What
resolved it was a fact outside both.

### ⛔⛔ There is no OR across item codes anywhere in Vintage Story

The rule reads *"iiex machines accept nails or rivets"*. Checked properly: a grid ingredient names one code
family, and an RCC stage's `requireStacks` is an **AND** list (`tryConsumeIngredients` walks every entry).
So substitution is **one recipe per fastener**, which is what the two gear routes already do.

★ Owner chose **six sites** - the four plated pipe segments, the tall hopper, the plated molten barrel -
rather than all thirteen. The scope is the point: past six the duplication starts multiplying against the
gear loop, and `FastenerSubstitutionTests` pins both halves, including a **control** (the twin-tub blower
stays nails-only) so a careless widening reads as a failure rather than as a quieter pass.

### The boilers are riveted now, and it is mass-neutral

Cornish 16 nail bundles → **32 rivets**; Lancashire 24 → **48**. Same metal either way, by the owner's
ruling: the gate is the change, not the price - a boiler that got *cheaper* for being riveted would make the
rivet route a reward rather than a requirement.

★★ **`PressureVesselGate` asserts a negative, which almost nothing else here does.** Every other definition
check asks that something resolves; a widening that let nails back into a boiler stage would satisfy all of
them. ⛔ Its premise is asserted first and had to be - the first version walked
`attributes.construction.stages`, which does not exist (stages ride on the `ExRightClickConstructable`
behaviour), so it found nothing and would have passed the negative while proving nothing.

⛔ `RequireRivets` takes the rivet's code as well as the hint domain: **the mod that ships the rivet is not
always the mod building with it**, and siex's boiler asking for `siex:rivet` would have wanted an item
nothing registers.

### Doc surgery, inverted

U8.11 says to strike the die family. The ruling inverts that step, so what landed instead:
`heading-machine.md` records the resolution and shrinks to bearing balls; `nail-machine.md`'s "1 x 1 x 1,
none in the file" claims are corrected to the built four-cell footprint and its two clips; a new
`rivet-machine.md` owns the riveter, which had no page at all.

## 2026-08-21 - The fastener benches: two rolled products stop being dead ends

U8.5 and U8.6. The mill and the shear have made nail plate and rivet rod for a week and **nothing consumed
either** - both were products with no sink. Now a nail cutter and a riveter eat them. Gate 9/9, **4,405** per
version (from 4,371).

### One machine, two dies - the owner's ruling taken literally

★★ *"All of the machining machines are the same machine with different shape, interaction cells and recipes
loaded."* So there is **one** blocktype, `forming-{nailcutter,riveter}`, one block class and one block
entity, and the only thing that makes a bench a nail cutter is a `type` variant it reads its own machine key
off. Nothing about either machine is written down twice.

★★ **The die is what names the job, and that is load-bearing.** `ItemDie` shipped in exlib as part of the
extensibility layer and had **no production consumer until now** - only tests. A die carries a whole
`ProcessJobSet` on the item, including which machine it belongs to, so a nail die in a riveter is refused
rather than quietly working. A third party adds a bench job by shipping a die: no table of ours to patch, no
naming blessing.

⛔ The plan said the opposite - U8.5 Step 5: *"there is no die and no tooling slot - state.md drops the
ItemDie family entirely"*. That was true on 2026-07-30 and false by 2026-08-12, when the machining line
ruled *"the heading, nail and rivet benches take dies"* and `ItemDie` was built. **Follow the design page,
not the plan step.**

### The mass ledger, which is the only interesting number here

- nail plate 100 u → **4** bundles at vanilla's 25 u each. Exact.
- rivet rod 25 u → **2** bundles at **12.5 u**. Exact.

★ 12.5 is fractional on purpose. The ruling set the rivet bundle at half a nail bundle - that is the whole
trade the substitution rule sells, **8 rivets per 100 u against nails' 4** - and the quantum is fixed by
what feeds it. Rounding to 12 or 13 would mint or lose metal on every stroke, and the ledger is worth more
than a round number. ★ The nail cutter is therefore the **cheap** recipe deliberately: once a shop can
rivet, nails are dominated everywhere both are accepted, so build cost is the only reason the nail route
survives.

### ⛔⛔ Two defects the guards caught, both in tooling rather than in the machine

1. **`convert-shape.py` closed only one direction of an animation loop.** `close_loop` copied the first
   keyframe's pose onto the last, so an element posed at frame 0 but not at the end was handled - and an
   element posed only in the *middle* was not. The riveter's `RiviterTopDie` is keyframed 12 → 34 and at
   neither end, which makes the animator hold its mid-clip pose from the loop point and then jump: a hitch
   at the same place every cycle. `LoopingAnimationTests` caught it on the first export. The rule is now
   symmetric - an element's **earliest** pose is written to both ends - which for an element already at
   frame 0 is exactly what the old rule did, so nothing shipped moves.
2. ⛔ The guard's own message is misleading when an element is at neither end: it prints *"keyframed at 59
   but not 0"* for an element that is at neither. The finding was right and the wording was not; left as
   found, noted here.

### Also

★ **`BlockEntityMpBench` extracted.** The shear's membership, stroke clock, network reads, ejection and
stroke persistence were all going to be copied wholesale; they are a base class now and the shear derives
from it. ⛔ The stroke key moved `shearRemaining` → `benchRemaining`, so the base reads the legacy key as a
fallback - a shear caught mid-stroke by the upgrade finishes its cut instead of stranding the piece in its
slot with no clock to clear it.

⛔ **The drive connector sits on the principal, not on the drawn shaft cell.** machines.txt puts the shaft on
each bench's `M`/`m` cell; both benches take drive at the principal the way the shear does, because
`BEBehaviorMPFillerPort` is a **vanilla-MP** intake (the flywheel's bridge) and not an mpenergy connector.
Cosmetic rather than functional, and it is the same simplification the shear already ships - all three want
re-homing together when the station family lands.

⛔ **fasteners.md's *"heading bench + rivet die"* row is superseded.** machines.txt gives the riveter a
machine of its own, consuming rivet rod, and that is what is drawn and what is built. The heading bench
(bolts, bearing balls) stays unbuilt, and bolts were rejected outright by the owner.

## 2026-08-21 - U7.1: the reheat furnace puts heat into stock, and the mill's deck was mirrored

The forming loop closes. The mill refuses a piece below rolling heat and nothing could reheat one, so a
schedule of round trips dead-ended at the first piece that went cold; a lit reheat hearth now soaks the bed.
Gate 9/9, **4,371** per version (from 4,335).

### The soak, and the law under it

★★ **`A/V` is the whole model, and it needed nothing new on the piece.** Heat crosses a solid at its
surface, so `RollingPass.AreaOverVolume(w, t)` is `2/t + 2/w` - which collapses to the two figures the
design quotes without either being written down: `2/t` for a piece wide against its gauge, `4/t` for a
square bar. The section falls out of the two dimensions a piece already carries, so the soak needed **no
shape class** - worth saying, because the `square | flat` property is still missing from code and every
grooved figure in the design docs is unreachable for want of it.

★ **`ReheatRateK` is calibrated, not chosen.** Vanilla's forge gains a work item 1500 C per game hour
(`BEForge.OnCommonTick200ms`), about 36 s from cold to rolling heat at the default calendar speed. At
`0.02` a bar takes ~40 s, a rod ~27, a slab ~58 - forge rate per piece, with **no x2**. Under an area law a
multiplier would put every piece under a forge and delete the beat; what the machine is for is that a forge
cannot hold a slab at all and the bed soaks three pieces for one fire.

★★ **The cooling changed with it, and that is the half that moves the game.** `RollingCoolRate` stopped
being a flat fraction and became the `k` that multiplies `A/V`. A flat rate made the last gap of a schedule
- the thinnest, longest, most exposed the piece ever is - the *cheapest* to hold hot.

⛔ Hosted on `SmeltCycle` rather than on a listener of the hearth's own, so the soak rides the core's
bounded away-catch-up. A hearth ticking itself looks identical while the chunk is loaded and teleports the
soak the moment it is not - and no headless test can tell the two apart.

### ⛔⛔ Two live defects the tests found on the way, neither of them new

1. **This furnace's hearth handle had always pointed at a fire slab.** `ShaftCentre` was `(-2, 0, 1)`
   against a drawing whose `H` is at `(-2, 0, 0)`; the offset had been computed against an
   `Origin(-6, -1)` that later became `-2`, and the class comment still quoted the old one. Nothing noticed
   because nothing had ever resolved this furnace's bed. ★ **A hand-declared offset into a drawing is a
   citation, and citations rot** - the fix restates which cell of which layer it names, and
   `ReheatSoakTests` pins it at all four facings.
2. ⛔⛔ **The mill's deck read mirrored at `ns`, in two ways that cancelled at one cell.** `AlongBarrel`
   swapped the axis (`localX = dz`) where the mill's own `RotateOffset` convention at 90 demands `-dz`,
   *and* rotated the within-cell hit point without centring it - so the click landed on the far side of its
   own cell. The two errors cancel at exactly the deck cell beside the stand, which is the cell every test
   and every casual play-test clicks. At the other two the widest gap - the only one fresh stock can enter
   at - sat under the player's hand instead of at the far end, and no click could reach it. Fixed with
   `ExOrientation.UnrotateXZ`, the inverse expressed as the opposite turn so there is **one** rotation table
   and not two; mutation-checked (`we` green, `ns` red under the old reading).

★ The plan flagged the sign inversion as "not verified fixed; check before assuming". It was right to, and
it understated the bug.

### ⛔ What the soak revealed: three forms cannot be reheated at all

`HeatingHearthLayout.StockOf` admits the two wrought forms and the three cast ones, so **`stock-rod`,
`stock-beam` and `stock-heavyplate` have no bed art and are refused**. Nor is a vanilla forge an escape: it
takes `ingot`, `metalplate`, `workitem` or a collectible declaring `forgable`, and **no item in this repo
declares it**. A piece that goes cold on those routes can still be sheared - shearing is temperature-free -
but it can never be rolled on, so a claimed beam cannot become plate once it cools. Three ways out and none
free: bed art for the missing forms, `forgable` on the small ones (which undercuts the machine's whole
argument), or the crosswise seating. Filed, not chosen.

### Also

Breaking a loaded heating hearth used to eat up to three unique work pieces; `OnBlockBroken` drops them
now. ⛔ The same hole is still open on the **puddling** hearth and is *not* the same fix - that bed stores
pig and fettle as counts rather than as stacks, so returning them means minting rather than handing back.

`LoadFireboxes` moved out of `FireboxTickTests` into `FurnaceLayoutRig`, where both suites reach it.

⛔ **A fixture caught reading its own copy.** `ItemSlot.TakeOut` hands over a *clone*, so a test that laid a
piece on the bed and then measured the stack it still held was measuring something the furnace never
touched - three assertions passed at 20 C. Read state back out of the machine, never off the thing you fed
it.

### U7.10 is blocked by a later ruling, not by work

The plan's last U7 task is "make the roll sets craftable" with a grid recipe off a cast blank. **The
machining line settled otherwise**: roll sets are **lathe-turned** from cast roller blanks, and the art
agrees - `item-lathed-rollers-*.json` and `item-sandcast-rollerblanks.json` are both drawn. So the lathe is
the mill's missing supplier, and a grid recipe now would contradict a settled design and be deleted when it
lands. ⛔ Building only the cast blank half is the `ClearBed` trap in advance: an item nothing consumes.
Every roll set therefore stays creative-only, which is the one thing standing between the forming line and a
survival demonstration.

## 2026-08-21 - Eight rulings after U6, two of which were defects

Asked for the open decisions and given all eight in one pass. Gate 9/9, **4,335** per version.

⛔⛔ **Two of the eight were not questions at all - they were bugs I had shipped that morning.**

1. **A drawn ball left the hearth at 500 C and shingling needs 750.** I had set it to
   `MoltenMetal.GlowMinTemp` because it read as "hot", without checking it against `ItemPuddledBall.CanWork`
   (`meltingPoint / 2`). The ball could not be used for the one thing it exists for. **Ruled: it leaves at
   the bath's own temperature**, which is `BlockEntityFurnaceCore.InternalTemperature` read live - so a
   furnace barely holding its process temperature hands out a cooler ball than one running properly, and
   the reheat furnace stays optional for the wrought route rather than becoming mandatory. ★ **A constant
   that reads as "hot" is not a temperature**: check it against whatever gate consumes it.
2. **The rabble and the paddle had no recipe** - both creative-only, so nothing in survival could operate
   the machine. Nothing caught it: the cost-catalogue guard covers the **blocks** a grid recipe outputs,
   never the items. **Ruled: a grid recipe each** (rod + plate + hammer, the plate's cell marking which end
   the tool carries).

★★ **The converter now unwraps `Root`, which makes the hearth's art pipeline usable again.** A top-level
group named `Root` is replaced by its children **in place** - the first attempt only fired when `Root` was
alone, and this editable has three siblings, so it silently shipped `Root/Base/*` paths against code
pruning on `Base/*`. Re-exporting brought the author's newer pig drawing with it: the nine pigs are 3
voxels tall rather than 2 and slightly repositioned, and their `Cube33..41` sub-cubes are gone. **No
element name the code references moved**, which `FurnacePartsTests` confirms.

★ **The bath now freezes when the fire goes out.** `ExtinguishResidue` sets it solid: nothing more can be
gathered and the bed has to be raked and started over. Balls already lying on the bed are solid iron and
survive, and a bed with no bath cannot freeze - otherwise a player could rake a bed that never held
anything and collect the cinder anyway.

★ **One cooldown covers both working strokes** (`PuddlingStrokeCooldownSec`, 3 s). The two verbs share it
because they are the same motion at the same door, and pacing them together is what stops a heat's
thirty-two gestures collapsing into thirty-two clicks. ⛔ Deliberately **not serialised**: a cooldown that
resets on reload costs nothing and keeps a transient out of the save.

★ **The ball wears bright ingot iron**, not the tarnished sheet older stock wears. Done properly rather
than by hand-editing the shipped file: a new `ironingot` key in the converter's map, the editables renamed
onto it, and the item def pointed at the same texture so shape and def cannot disagree.

**Kept unchanged, confirmed good:** the melt rate (0.08 per cycle, a full bed down in ~2 minutes) and the
ball at 200 u (16 balls + 16 draw-outs per heat). **Not walked in game** - the owner chose to push on to U7
rather than stop and verify.

## 2026-08-21 - U6.8-U6.11: the puddling furnace runs a whole heat, and U6 closes

The second half of U6, and the machine now does the thing it exists for: fettle, charge, fire, melt down,
rabble, draw out, clean, shingle. 9/9 targets, **4,331** per version (+50 cases on the morning's 4,281).

### U6.8 - melt down

★★ **The owner's art ruling was smaller than either option offered.** *"When hearth smelts pigs those are
removed and instead a molten metal surface is drawn. When player paddles, they pull ball out as part of
paddle shape, no need to render ball shapes inside."* So the hearth needed **one** new element group - a
bath lying across the bed - and **no ball group at all**. R7 is satisfied because the ball appears on the
paddle, whose drawn `IronBall1` head is exactly that and is why it was drawn holding one. The bath went
into both the shipped shape and its editable source as three slabs, one per bed cell, on the existing
`iron` texture.

⛔⛔ **The editable hearth shape cannot be re-exported as it stands.** Its elements are nested under a
`Root` group the shipped file does not have, and `convert-shape.py` does not unwrap it - so a re-export
would ship `Root/Base/*` paths while `PuddlingHearthLayout` prunes on `Base/*`. `ExShapeElements.Pruned`
drops an unknown name **without raising anything**, so the hearth would render as nothing at all with every
test still green. `FurnacePartsTests` pins the element names, which is what would catch it; the divergence
itself is unfixed and is now an open item.

★ **The melt is driven only from `SmeltCycle`.** The hearth registers no listener of its own, so it rides
the core's bounded away-catch-up instead of teleporting the heat on reload - the trap the plan names, and
one that looks identical in every headless test.

⛔ **The reach rule turned out to have a loading order nothing stated.** A fettled centre blocks both
flanks, so a bed can only be filled **flanks first, centre last** - the mirror of the centre-first order
balls come out in. The fixture hit it before the test did.

### U6.9 - rabbling

Both tools exist now (`iiex:tool-rabble`, `iiex:tool-paddle`), exported through `convert-shape.py` from art
that had been drawn and unused. The rabble gathers, the paddle draws out, and both are gated on the **small
working door** - which is also why `IsVenting` changed meaning to the main door only.

⛔ **Step 8's defect was real and is fixed**: the hearth matched held items on `Code.Path` alone, so any mod
shipping an item pathed `pig` charged it. It now matches domain and path together, with the domain read off
the block rather than written down.

### U6.10 - draw out and clean out

`ClearBed` has its caller at last - empty-handed on a worked-out bed - and it returns the spent fettling
with **exactly 3 tap cinder** (owner's ruling). That closes the loop precisely: `FettleRecipeDefinitions`
already turns 3 cinder into 3 fettle, and 3 fettle is what the next heat's three rows want, so a running
furnace never needs ore for its bed again.

★ **The mass identity is the test, not the count.** Nine pigs at 375 u make 3375 u, which divides into 16
balls of 200 u with 175 u left over. Asserted as
`balls x BallUnits + cinderUnits == 9 x PigUnits` rather than as `Assert.Equal(16, ...)`, which would have
passed at the pig's old 150 u too and said nothing.

### U6.11 - the helve, and a furnace the player can build

⛔⛔ **The plan's "no precedent in the mod" was right about the mod and wrong about the game.** Vanilla's
`BlockEntityAnvil.TryPut` already hands `IAnvilWorkable.TryPlaceOn` the anvil and keeps the existing work
item when one stands, so a workable that adds voxels rather than replacing them **accumulates for free** -
that is exactly how vanilla's own ingots pile. No patch, no new machinery.

What is this mod's is the **dedicated work item**: a pile of balls becomes `iiex:shingleworkitem-iron`
rather than vanilla's `workitem-iron`, because iron has dozens of smithing recipes and the pile must have
exactly one. With one match the anvil selects it without a dialog, and that absence of a mode switch is the
mechanic - whatever is piled, a bar is the only thing it can make.

★ **The pile and the target are the same voxel count**, 160 either way: two balls of 80, and a
2 x 5 x 16 solid. `FullyWorkable` therefore sheds nothing and the iron is conserved exactly. A ball's 80
voxels are its 200 u at the density the pig already sets (2.5 u per vx³), so nothing is minted by moving
metal between them.

⛔ **A silent-loss trap caught in the writing**: `ExRecipeDef.GridObject` sets a *single* recipe object and
`Grid` appends to an array. Chaining `GridObject` then `Grid` leaves `Count` at 1 and the other seven
recipes vanish with nothing raised. All eight became `Grid`.

★★ **The reverberatory chassis is craftable - eight recipes in one file, deliberately.** Both cores, both
hearths, both doors, the damper and the **firebox**, which is a required cell in both layouts and had no
recipe at all: without it neither machine could be built by a player. They are together because grid-pattern
collision is a property of the *file*: two recipes sharing a pattern with overlapping ingredients collide
and the loser silently never resolves, so a clash authored a unit apart would surface only after the first
had blessed its golden with nothing red in between.

### U6's gate, as far as a headless run can walk it

The filler-accounting invariant passes for all four furnace layouts in both directions; no firebox carries
a disruption floor above its own capacity; `StackDraughtTests` pins a curve that rises, peaks and declines;
a `StructureRig`-raised puddling furnace completes itself with nothing forced, resolves its own hearth at
all four facings, lights, holds, crosses into Melting and melts a full charge down on the clock; the
ball/bar mass conservation holds; and all three suites are green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22.

⛔ **What no test here can see** is what it looks like: the bath, the two tool items, the working strokes on
the door, and whether 16 gestures per heat is the right number. That is an in-game walk.

## 2026-08-21 - U6.1-U6.7: the puddling furnace melts, and B8 closes on all five counts

**The reverberatory furnaces have never worked**, and by the end of this stretch both of them do. Seven
tasks, 9/9 targets, **4,281** per version (+55 cases). U6.8-U6.11 - the puddling cycle itself - are still
ahead.

### U6.1 - a furnace nobody could build

★★ **The test had to be at definition level, and the plan was right about why.** `StructureRig.Raise`
stands a block up in every empty footprint cell, fillers included, so a scenario completes a furnace no
player could build and this entire defect class is invisible to it. `FurnaceFillerAccountingTests` compares
the cells a layout declares `exlib:structurefiller` against the cells its parts emit through their own
`fillerOffsets`, rotated by each part's own facing, **in both directions** - an orphan means the structure
can never complete, a stray means a filler lands outside the footprint. It failed with exactly the three
findings the plan predicted and passed the blast furnace and cupola untouched, which is the control.

Both hearths were missing their second course - the low roof over the bed - so `.Layer(1, ...)` went onto
each footprint. The fourth finding, the chimney cap's stray filler, waited for U6.5.

### U6.2 - the raw-key HUD bug, which had moved

⛔ **Step 1's two named instances were already fixed; three others were live.** Doing the verification
honestly rather than trusting the plan turned up `iiex:furnace-chargepile-help-take` and the two
`furnace-heatinghearth-help-*` keys, all carrying a `furnace-` prefix the lang files do not, all rendering
raw in game.

⛔⛔ **And a second family nothing had ever checked: six `SendIngameError` codes.** The hearths' six
`iiex-hearth-*` rows were keyed bare, while every other error in the file carries `game:ingameerror-`.
`SendIngameError(code)` with no message resolves `ingameerror-{code}` in the **game** domain, so all six
rendered raw - every refusal the two hearths can give.

★★ **The structural fix is a call-site scan, not only the constant migration.** The plan called
`LangCoverage`'s blindness to call sites "a permanent hole" and named the `IiexLang` migration as the only
fix; the migration is compiler-enforced but only where it is applied, and 130 hand-typed sites remain.
`LangCallSites` scans `Lang.Get`, `ActionLangCode` and one-argument `SendIngameError` for literal keys and
checks each against every locale, normalising the way vanilla's `TranslationService` does on load (a bare
key gains the domain, a qualified one is verbatim, an error code becomes `game:ingameerror-{code}`).
Concatenated keys and the two-argument error overload are skipped, both being families rather than keys.
Wired into all three domains; all three are clean. **Proved by mutation**: reverting one fix turns it red
in all three locales, naming the file.

### U6.3 - B8's fifth cause: a firebox cannot hold a shaft's disruption floor

⛔⛔ `DisruptionMixFloor` was a flat `144` on the core, never overridden. A firebox holds `cells x 12` -
**12** puddling, **24** reheat - so a lit hearth was at once full enough to light and under the floor,
counted a disruption every tick and went out on the 30-second grace. **`MaxFuelBurnTime` was unreachable
and had always been.**

Fixed on the branch and **sealed**: `DisruptionMixFloor => ChargeCapacityUnits x FireboxDisruptionFloorFraction`.
It still bites from the other side - a firebox lights full and burns on a clock rather than by consumption,
so the only way under the floor is raking fuel back out of a lit box, which is the mechanic it was always
meant to be. `FurnaceBranchGuards` gained the law; QW2's test fired its own notice and was rewritten.

★★ **The first thing the fix uncovered: the reheat furnace reaches its melt phase**, which no reverberatory
hearth in this mod had ever done. Its `MeltingPoint` is `RollingTempC`, well inside what a firebox reaches -
only the inherited floor stood between it and working.

### U6.4 - the losses become per-machine

`ChargeLossFull` and `TransferLoss` are virtuals on the core, defaulting to today's behaviour so the shaft
furnaces are bit-identical. A firebox pays 100 C for a bed rather than the shaft's 310 for a descending
column, and a reverberatory one pays a bridge loss on top. ⛔ **`TransferLoss` is deliberately not a branch
constant**: the crucible furnace is a firebox machine whose pots stand in the coke with no bridge, and a
constant would make it unbuildable later. `HeatBalance` gained the contributor and the firebox branch a HUD
line for it, so the total is never larger than the terms accounting for it.

### U6.5 + U6.6 - the draught, and B8's other half

⛔⛔ **The owner ruled the puddling furnace's chimney is FIXED** - *"it is fixed in layout because of the
cap"* - which settles the plan's open question the other way from its own recommendation and **dissolves the
counted stack walk**. `StackCourses` is `LocalCellsWithRole(CellRole.Flue).Count`, read off the drawing: no
walk, no cache, no neighbour invalidation, and `ComponentScanBelow` never enters it. Steps 4, 7 and 9 fell
away; the walk arrives with U9's coke oven and crucible furnace, which do have player-built stacks. The cap
moved from `(-1,7,0)` to `(0,7,0)`, over the flue it caps, which closed U6.1's stray.

`StackDraught.NaturalDraughtFor(courses, damperOpen, venting)` is pure and shared. Its tests pin the
**shape** - rises, peaks, declines, and 30 courses are worse than none - never the coefficients, which are
all proposals to calibrate in play.

★ **`IsVenting` changed meaning: the main door only.** Not dumping the heat is the entire reason the small
working door exists, and rabbling happens through it - if it vented, the bath would cool on every one of the
sixteen gestures.

**`PuddlingProcessTempC = 1400`**, and the arithmetic works out with the drawn four courses:

| | natural | T_in | T_process |
|---|---|---|---|
| four courses, damper open | 0.704 | 1741.6 | **1421.6** |
| no stack at all | 0.500 | 1512.5 | 1192.5 |
| damper shut | 0.246 | 1227.1 | 907.1 |
| main door open | 0.422 | 1425.0 | 1105.0 |

Twenty-one degrees of headroom, and every way of losing the draught takes it away. ★ **The transfer loss
landed at 100 C rather than the design's proposed 250, and the curve is why**: 250 was computed against a
draught model that saturated at 0.85, giving T_in 1906; the settled peaking curve at four courses gives
1741.6, so the bridge loss comes down to keep the T_process the design intended. The ruling anticipated
exactly this - *"if 1400 needs more courses than a player will build, the losses move, not this number."*

⛔ **One law over-reached and was narrowed.** "The chimney must be load-bearing" is true of the puddling
furnace and false of the reheat furnace, whose stack is a heat-treatment dial and which works stock at 900 C
far below its own ceiling. It moved out of the branch law into a puddling-specific case.

⛔ **`PeakCourses` shipped without a caller and was removed the same day.** With every reverberatory chimney
fixed, no player is choosing a stack height, so naming a peak in the readout would be noise. The test
computes it instead. It returns to production with U9 - which is exactly the `ClearBed` trap this plan warns
about, caught before it set.

**B8 is closed on all five counts**, and `A_puddling_furnace_crosses_into_its_melt_phase` is the proof.

### U6.7 - the core resolves its own hearth

`ScanForOutlets` resolves the bed at `ShaftCentrePos` alongside the damper and the door, so all three
refresh on exactly the schedule the taps do. ★ **Rotation is free** - `ShaftCentrePos` is
`GlobalOf(ShaftCentre)` - and the test proves it at all four facings. ⛔ The fixture's own bug on the way in:
the hearth's legend is orientation-pinned, so a furnace facing south wants its hearth coded `-s`; the
authored letter satisfies north alone.

## 2026-08-21 - The storage rack, and a design it superseded on the way in

**The owner drew a rack and specced it in one message**, and the spec turned out to overwrite a written
design. `stock-rack.md` (2026-08-15) sized a rack by *layers* - `piecesPerLayer = floor(rackWidth /
pieceWidth)` x `RackLayers`, with a worked capacity table - and named `PileLayout` as its blocking
prerequisite. The ruling is **length**: a row of N cells, each stored stack occupying a contiguous run of
L, and *"3 stacks of 1 block length, 1 stack of 2 + 1 stack of 1, or 1 stack of 3"* is the complete
enumeration for N = 3. Gate 9/9, **4,226** per version (+66 cases).

★★ **That dissolved the blocker rather than clearing it.** The pyramid/flat modes, the half-width offset,
the 0.866 y-step, the layer count and the jitter all belong to the reheat hearth's bed; with nothing
stacking upward the rack needs none of them, so it was built standalone and stage 1 of
`2026-08-15-item-piles.md` is still unstarted and still only the hearth's. ★ **Search the design docs
before building to a fresh spec** - the conflict was in prose nothing checks, and building to the message
alone would have quietly contradicted a page that is still cited by the reheat furnace.

**It also answered two of that page's own Open questions**: mixed contents (yes, by length) and one
inventory or per-layer (one ordered list of runs).

★ **Two things are config because the owner said everything is.** *How many cells* is the blocktype's own
footprint - `BlockStorageRack.LocalCells` reads it off `fillerOffsets` and orders it along whichever
horizontal axis varies - so a longer rack is another definition and no code moves. *What an item occupies*
is `config/bayoccupancy/*.json`, a contributed-to catalogue in the same shape as the process ones: a mod
makes its stock rackable by shipping a file, a second rule for one item is reported and the first stands,
and the most specific rule wins either way round so load order cannot decide a capacity.

★ **The catalogue is also the whitelist.** An unlisted item sizes to null, not to one - a store that
defaulted would be a chest, and a rack has to draw whatever it holds.

⛔ **The renderer is the part no headless test can see, so it was copied rather than invented.** Vanilla's
`BlockEntityDisplay` already solves "a block that draws arbitrary items": build the meshes on the **main
thread** (Initialize and every content change), cache one per load, and let `OnTesselation` do nothing but
add them with a transform. That is what keeps atlas insertion off the chunk worker, and it is why the BE
implements `ITexPositionSource` against the **block** atlas - an item tesselated through the item atlas
carries UVs addressing the wrong sheet. The frame stays the default block mesh, so `OnTesselation` returns
`base` rather than `true`. Only the centring math is pinned by a test.

★ **The art frame and the footprint frame agree, and a test says so.** The rack is drawn running **-Z**
(bays at z 0..16, -16..0, -32..-16), so the footprint is authored -Z too and `StructureAngle` takes no
180 - the workbench's `+180` belongs to how *that* art was drawn, not to megablocks in general.
`The_mesh_and_the_footprint_turn_together` is the check, borrowed from `WorkbenchTests`.

★ **`allowAttach` is on** (owner's call): racks stack vertically, so shelving is just racks and the next
rack up must land on any cell of the one below. The cost is that torches and vines can hang on the
invisible cells.

**Five gates caught it on the way in**, all of them the repo's own: a BE that stores an `ItemStack` must
carry the collectible-mapping pair, a craftable block needs a cost-catalogue row, `IiexBlocks.g.cs` needed
regenerating, and both new defs needed goldens.

⛔ **The shipped occupancy numbers are a starting table**, grounded in `StockForm.BaseLength` rounded up to
whole cells. `caststock-slab` is the only 3-cell entry, so it alone exercises the whole-rack arrangement -
worth an in-game eye before it settles.

## 2026-08-21 - U4.9: a shaft furnace stops lighting itself, and U4 closes

**A charged, blown, structurally sound shaft now sits dark.** It catches when a player reaches a lit flame
through an open tap-hole: `BlockEntityShaftFurnace.BlownIn`, set by `TryLightFromTap`, cleared when the
furnace goes out. The sequence the design has wanted since 2026-08-05 is buildable end to end - break the
plug, torch it, re-plug, blast on - and it costs one clay plug a campaign. Gate 9/9, **4,160** per version.
That closes **U4** entirely.

⛔⛔ **The plan's own rescoping note was wrong about the code, and its Step 1 caution is what caught it.**
Written 2026-08-05, before U3.6 landed, it said the automatic "charge-is-full" branch lives on the shared
core and must be pushed down onto the two branches. It is **already firebox-only**: the shaft sets
`DerivesState => true` and never reaches it. The shaft's auto-ignition was in `DeriveState`, which returned
Firing the moment a raceway course held carbon. Nothing needed pushing down. ★ **Read the code the task
consumes before believing the task's summary of it**, even when the summary is recent and specific.

★★ **So the work was adding the one thing that branch was designed not to have.** `BlockEntityShaftFurnace`
had no "was lit" bit on purpose - its own docstring said so - and the reason it could not have one is the
reason it can: burn-out retains no fuel at the raceway (`BfBurnoutFuelRetainedBottom` is 0), so there is
nothing for a stale flag to relight. The flag is cleared in `ExtinguishResidue`, the same event that burns
the columns out, which makes blowing in exactly once per campaign.

★ **The torch asks none of `DeriveState`'s questions.** It sets the latch and stops. "Will it light" and
"is it still alight" stay one question asked in one place, so torching a shaft with no carbon in front of
its tuyeres buys nothing - which is a case rather than a comment.

★ **The predicate is vanilla's own `BlockBehaviorCanIgnite`**, which every `*-lit-*` block carries, rather
than a torch code. Deliberately not `ItemFirestarter`: vanilla treats that as a separate class of igniter,
and a bow drill does not reach two metres up a tap-hole.

⛔ **The ripple was 58 tests** - every scenario that expected a furnace to light itself. Paid with the
fixture lever again: `BlowInRig` performs the ritual through the tap block's **own interaction**, so a
scene that lit its furnace by reaching past the gesture would stop passing when the gesture broke. Each rig
gained a `BlowIn()` step; the smex rig takes `blowIn: true` in its constructor because twenty-five of its
scenarios are about combustion and one is about ignition.

⛔⛔ **Two of the three furnace rigs identified as neither side.** `EnumAppSide` has no zero member
(`Server = 1`), so a substitute's default `Side` matched neither branch - and every `Side == Server` block
in the production tick was being skipped in the cupola and smex suites without a word. Found because the
blow-in click is server-gated and silently did nothing there. Both rigs are server-side now, and both
suites stayed green, which is the only reason to believe the gap had not been hiding a failure too.

★ **Two "on its own" claims were retired rather than patched.** smex's
`A_built_and_blown_furnace_reaches_melting_on_its_own` is now
`A_built_and_blown_furnace_stays_dark_until_a_torch_reaches_it` and performs the gesture; the hopper's
`..._FUELLED_and_LIT_through_its_own_hopper` says what it actually shows - the hopper lays fuel the raceway
will burn, and a player brings the flame.

★ **A shaft's save now differs from a hearth's by its columns *and one bit*.** `ShaftColumnsTests` said the
difference was the column keys alone; it says columns plus `blownIn` now, which is the sharper statement: a
hearth catches by itself and has nothing to remember.

⛔ **The legacy default, again** (as U4.7's `plugged` was): a running furnace in an existing world carries
no `blownIn` key, and reading that as "never lit" would have put every one of them out on load - silently,
because the state is derived and the next tick would simply say Idle. The read is
`tree.GetBool("blownIn", State != FurnaceState.Idle)`.

**Docs corrected**: `ironmaking.md`'s ritual section is built end to end, `layered-charge.md` is down to the
lit front alone, and both furnace pages' "ignition is positional" prose now says a flame **and** a position.

## 2026-08-21 - U4.6-U4.8: the taps get their own art, and a clay plug for a closed state

**Three units, one landing, because they could not be separated.** Adopting the drawn tap shapes deletes
the pour animation whether or not you mean to - neither new shape has an `animations` array - so U4.6 and
U4.7 were always one change. U4.8 came with them because U4.7 rewrites `OnBlockInteractStart` wholesale and
the replacement has no canal check in it: keeping the no-canal gate would have meant writing code the same
landing deletes. Gate 9/9, **4,151** per version.

**Each tap draws its own shape now.** `iiex:furnace/irontap` and `iiex:furnace/slagtap`, exported from the
editables; the shared `furnace/tap` is deleted. ★ The art is what carries the notch heights - the iron
channel runs at model Y 2-3 and the cinder channel at Y 10-11 - so **both taps belong at layout y=1 and the
course between them is drawn, not built**. Both iwex furnaces already draw them that way.

★ **The rotation offset stays 0, which the plan flagged as the place to get it wrong invisibly.** Both new
shapes run their launder out past z=16 (`TapCanal/Cube8` reaches z=20 on the iron tap, z=24 on the slag
tap), the same direction the old art pointed and the direction a tap declared `-n` pours. Nothing to
reverse. `TryPourMetal` was not touched.

**The clay plug is the closed state.** A tap arrives plugged; an empty hand breaks the plug out, fire clay
(`TapPlugClayCost`, 4) stops it again. No animator: `OnTesselation` prunes the shape to
`Base`/`TapCanal`(`/ClayPlug`), the same `ExShapeElements.Pruned` route the puddling hearth uses, which is
why `ClayPlug` had to be a top-level element in both files.

★★ **The open ruling settled: breaking a plug refunds nothing.** `TapUnplugClayRefund` ships at **0** where
`CanalUnsealClayRefund` is 2. ironmaking.md states the cost of a blow-in as *one whole clay plug*, which a
refund would halve, and a tap plug is knocked through rather than chiselled out whole. The knob exists, so
one number reverses it.

⛔ **A missing tree key defaults to the wrong state.** A tap saved before this unit carries `isPouring` and
no `plugged`, and `GetBool("plugged")` answers **false** - so a plain read would have reported every
existing tap OPEN, not stopped, and started draining crucibles on load. The read is
`tree.GetBool("plugged", !tree.GetBool("isPouring"))`. ★ Worth generalising: **renaming a saved flag to its
own negation makes the default the bug**, and nothing about it is visible in a fresh world.

★ **An open tap with no canal stopped being a refusal.** It used to raise `iiex:tap-err-nocanal` and refuse
to open, which is exactly what made the designed blow-in (open a tap, torch it, re-plug) unbuildable. The
same key is now a status line in the tap's block info, so a tap that delivers nothing says why.

⛔ **A class doc comment over 16 lines fails `CommentStyleGuards`**, which is how the first pass went red.
The rationale moved to docs/design with a citation, which is what that guard is for.

⛔ **Two more things the plans got wrong about their own tooling.** `convert-shape.py` joins
`assets/editable/shapes` with the name verbatim, so an editable in a subfolder needs its subpath
(`furnaces/shaft/furnace-block-irontap`); and `EXLIB_WRITE_GOLDENS` wants `iiex/blocktypes/furnace/irontap`,
the def's own `domain/path`, as U4.5 found.

**Doc drift swept while here**, all of it created by U4.4/U4.5 and none of it caught by a guard:
`blast-furnace-cold.md` still named `BfMaxMoltenIron` / `BfMaxMoltenSlag` (deleted), still said extinguish
freezes the pool (it does not), and still listed the live crucible as open. Its Open list, the
`layered-charge.md` design-only list and `ironmaking.md`'s ritual section are corrected: **the plug half is
built, the torch half is U4.9**.

**Deferred to the smex remake, and now written into the drawing** (`BlockBlastFurnaceCoreHot`): its `S`
glyph is at y=2 while `T` is at y=1, so with the height in the art it draws its cinder notch a course above
the metal it skims and counts the offset twice. Moving `S` down is a layout change and a blessing, and it
belongs in the one remake that also separates `Pool` from `Chargeable`.

## 2026-08-21 - U4.5: the crucible is pool only, and four tests lost their subject

**`.Role('h', CellRole.Chargeable)` is gone from both iwex shaft furnaces.** The crucible course carries
`Pool` alone; the burden starts a course above it, level with the tuyeres. Cold furnace 39 -> 36 chargeable
cells, cupola 5 -> 4, and the two core goldens re-blessed to say so - a four-cell deletion under
`multiblockRoles.Chargeable` and nothing else. Gate 9/9, **4,136** per version.

★★ **The shipped shafts became uniform-floored, and that cost four tests their subject.** Every case that
distinguished "fill from the column's own floor" from "fill from the shaft box's" was written against the
cold furnace's ragged hearth row, which no longer exists: the uneven-floor region of
`ChargeMaterialisationTests`, `ChargePileTests`' block-index case, and the obstruction trio. They now run on
a shared `FurnaceLayoutRig.SteppedShaftDef` - two columns floored at y=1 and y=2 - and
`Both_shipped_shafts_are_uniform_floored` states the premise, so nobody later deletes the fixture as
redundant with the shipped drawing. ⛔ **A layout simplification silently retires coverage**, and the
production code (`ColumnFloorY`, `ChargeCellsOf`) is untouched and still needs it.

⛔ **The rig's glyph pair could not simply lose `HearthGlyph`**, which is what the plan's Step 2 asked for.
`AssertFurnaceGeometry` and `ChargeCells` are shared with the smex suite, whose furnace still charges its
crucible; hard-coding either set would have made one of the two suites assert nothing rather than fail.
Both take the glyphs from the caller now, and the several-codes form's "every listed glyph is used" half is
what keeps a wrong list red.

★ **The cold furnace holds 7.7 % less: 1248 -> 1152 units.** The heat balance's calibration table is
unmoved - every row saturates the charge-loss clamp, so the denominator is invisible to it - but a full
hearth really is smaller in play. Accepted on the same ground as the cupola's -20 %: the crucible was never
somewhere burden should rest.

★ **The hearth glyph still admits `furnace-chargepile`, deliberately.** A world saved before this change has
piles standing in its crucible, and a code that refused them would read the furnace as broken on load. The
role no longer marks those cells, so the sync walk ignores them, and `ClaimPoolCells` treats a pile holding
no rejected charge as free - so the next melt writes hearth metal over it and the leftover heals itself.
`The_role_answers_exactly_the_cells_the_charge_pile_code_does` states the gap and pins that it is exactly
the crucible.

⛔ **`EXLIB_WRITE_GOLDENS` does not take the path the plans give it.** The filter matches the def's own
`domain/path`, so the fragment is `iiex/blocktypes/furnace/blastcore`; a `test/.../goldens/` prefix matches
nothing and the run passes having written no file. Three plan steps carry the wrong form (U4.4 Step 12, U4.5
Step 5, U4.6 Step 7); U4.5's is corrected in place.

★ **A redundant fixture from U4.4, removed.** smex's near-copy of `HearthRig` was written on the belief that
a test project cannot reference another test project. It can, and does -
`SteelIndustryExpanded.Tests.csproj` already project-references the iiex suite and imports its namespace
project-wide, which is how the smex furnace tests reach `FurnaceLayoutRig`. The copy was shadowing the
shared one; deleted.

**Held deliberately: siex's hot blast furnace keeps the overlap.** Dropping `Chargeable` there alone would
leave a two-cell crucible under a 3x3 shaft and bake that into a golden, when the hearth's real defect is a
cinder notch a course too high - crucible, notch and tuyeres move together in the smex remake, one layout
change and one blessing. New `CrucibleOverlapTests` makes the deferral visible instead of free: it pins
that two of the nine columns draw a block short while the bath stands, that the units they hide still count
toward `ShaftChargeUnits`, and that two columns floor on a cell the pool owns. It touches no golden, and it
fails the day the remake starts.

**Not done in this unit:** U4.6 onward (the two drawn tap shapes, which land together with U4.7's clay plug).

## 2026-08-20 - U4.4: the hearth is live molten cells, and the pool leaves the furnace's save

**The two float pools are gone.** `_moltenIron` / `_moltenSlag` on `BlockEntityShaftFurnace` are replaced by
metal standing in `iiex:hearthmetal-*` blocks the furnace places into its layout's `CellRole.Pool` cells as
it melts, each hosting an iron cell and a slag cell (`hm_iron_` / `hm_slag_`). Deleted with them:
`_maxMoltenIron`/`_maxMoltenSlag`, `MaxMoltenProduct`/`MaxMoltenSlagPool` and the cupola's two overrides,
`ClearMoltenPools`, `DrainedMetalUnits`, `StampSolidProduct`, `SolidifyBottomLayer`, the
`"moltenIron"`/`"moltenSlag"` tree keys, and `BfMaxMoltenIron` / `BfMaxMoltenSlag` /
`CupolaMaxMoltenCastIron` / `CupolaMaxMoltenSlag`.

★★ **Extinguishing no longer freezes anything.** There is nothing to freeze: the metal was written into the
world as it was made, and the cells latch solid on their own thermal update. `ExtinguishResidue` is down to
`BurnOutCharge()`. ★ That deletes a whole class of bug rather than fixing one - the old path could score a
fuel band at the hearth as refused charge, find no cell to freeze into, and then zero the pool anyway, which
is what `Breaking_a_running_cupola_...` was written to catch. The case is kept, re-aimed at the guarantee
that survives: break a running cupola and every unit is still in its crucible.

⛔⛔ **A cell holds whole units and the old pool did not.** The melt renders a fractional yield, so pooling
`(int)product` quietly lost up to a unit of iron *and* of slag every cycle - a real yield cut over a
campaign, caught by smex's own `88.4 vs 88`. Fixed with a per-cycle carry (`_productCarry`/`_slagCarry`),
deliberately **not** serialized: it is worth under one unit, and a pool key on the furnace is exactly what
this unit removed. The yield tests now state the claim over pool **plus** carry, which is exact where
asserting the pool alone would accept a truncation.

⛔⛔ **`0 >= 0` read as a full crucible.** `LiquidCapacityReached` compares pooled units against
`PoolCells.Count * HearthUnitsPerBand`; a furnace whose drawing marks no pool cell - both hearths - then
reported itself permanently backed up and could never melt. Caught by `FireboxChargeTests`, whose whole
subject is that neither hearth can back up on its own output. The gate now tests the cell count first.

⛔ **`HearthSlagSpoutBand` is a height, not a capacity.** Reading it as one gave the slag cell 10 units and
broke the tap's own regression oracle (24 where 36 is pinned). The crucible is one shared volume - slag
floats on the metal - so both cells carry the same band; the spout height decides where slag *leaves*.

★★ **Two rewrites that were fixture work, not assertion work.** `ColdBlastFurnaceScenes.MoltenIron`,
`CupolaScenes.MoltenCastIron` and smex's `BlastFurnaceRig.MoltenIron` stopped reflecting a private float and
started summing the crucible floor, which left ~40 scenario assertions reading exactly as they did. The same
lever U2 used. A shared `HearthRig` carries the block, the factory and the sum; smex has a near-copy,
because a test project cannot reference another test project and the shared testing library cannot
reference a mod.

⛔ **A second registration under one block code shadows the first.** smex's extinguish test registered its
own `iiex:hearthmetal-pigiron` with a cell-less entity, so every push into it was refused and the pool read
zero while the blocks were visibly there. The tell was a passing block assertion beside a failing amount.

★ **Scenario claims that changed, and what replaced them.** *The pool freezes onto the hearth rather than
vanishing* asserted a nugget count derived through `BfUnitsPerSolidNugget`; it now asserts that every unit
that was liquid is still on the floor - the same claim, stated exactly instead of through a lossy
conversion. *Shutdown resets the heat and the pools* keeps the heat half and drops the pool half, because
going out is no longer a reason to destroy metal. The furnace's tree no longer round-trips a pool, which
`ShaftColumnsTests` now pins as "a shaft differs from a hearth by its charge columns alone".

⛔ **The plan's line numbers were all stale** - written against a ~500-line `BlockEntityShaftFurnace.cs` that
is 1103 lines now. Measured drift is recorded at the head of U4.4 in the plan. Everything the task
*consumes* was re-verified and all of it exists.

**Not done in this unit:** U4.5 (drop `Chargeable` from the hearth glyph) onward. The smex hot furnace is
still the one drawing where `Pool` and `Chargeable` share cells, which U4.5 Step 6 defers deliberately.

## 2026-08-20 - The workbench is BUILT: the art arrived and the bench stands on two cells

**The owner drew it** - `assets/editable/shapes/machines/machine-megablock-workbench.json`, 55 elements,
footprint `O #`, the same two cells the design table occupies - and ruled the two open questions on it:
the vices stay where they are, and the footprint is two blocks. Built the same day: `BlockWorkbench`,
`BlockEntityWorkbench`, `GuiDialogWorkbench`, the shape copy-out, the grid recipe, three locales, the cost
row and 12 tests.

★★ **The bench body is exactly two cells and only the vices leave them.** Measured with the relative-child
rule: the trestle rails, the top and the tool shelf sit inside X 0..32 / Y 0..16 / Z 0..16, while the east
vice reaches X 38.1 and both vice screws pass Z 16. Ruled acceptable by the owner - a vice hangs off the
end of a bench, and the cells it hangs into are air. ⛔ Worth knowing rather than re-deriving: two benches
placed side by side will interpenetrate by those 6 voxels.

★★ **The mesh and the footprint are two separate declarations and a test now holds them together.** The
shape carries `rotateYByType` and the block carries `StructureAngle`; a bench whose model faced one way
while its reserved cell lay the other would put a solid invisible cell in the open and hang half the drawn
bench in a buildable space. `WorkbenchTests.The_mesh_and_the_footprint_turn_together` compares the two per
side and catches dropping the +180 offset from either.

★ **The art's frame is the `s` variant**, because the vices mount on its +Z edge and that is the edge the
player works from - so both the shape rotation and the footprint carry +180 over `AngleFromSide`, as the
casting bed already does.

★★ **The grid needed no recipe engine, as designed** - `GridRecipe.Matches`/`ConsumeInput` take a
`gridWidth` and scan sub-positions, so the bench passes 5 and every existing recipe matches inside it. What
the bench owns is the **output distribution**: a result larger than one stack spreads across the output row,
partial stacks fill before empty ones so a held Craft button does not open a slot per craft, and a simulate
pass refuses a craft *before* the grid is consumed so a full bench cannot eat its inputs.

★ **`OnCreatedByCrafting` is called, which the superset claim needs.** A collectible may write onto its own
output as it is made - a pie takes its filling from the slots, an omni-rotatable block its facing - so
skipping the hook would have made the bench craft *nearly* what the player's grid does. Given through a
scratch slot, because the hook works on one output slot while a bench result may fill several.

⛔⛔ **`GridRecipe.Matches` gained the world parameter in 1.22.** 1.20 and 1.21 take
`(IPlayer, ItemSlot[], int)`; 1.22 takes `(IPlayer, IWorldAccessor, ItemSlot[], int)`. Shimmed in
`LegacyApi` behind `!GAME_GE_1_22` like the rest, arity separating it from the instance method it forwards
to. `ConsumeInput` is identical on all three.

⛔ **`ItemStack.Equals(world, other, ignored)` is not headless-safe.** It defers to
`CollectibleObject.Equals`, which reads `api.World` off the collectible's own captured API rather than the
world it was handed - null for a test-registered item, so it NREs. The merge check compares the collectible
and the attribute trees directly, passing the world through.

⛔ **A shipped shape was moved into `assets/editable/` rather than copied**, which deleted
`assets/iiex/shapes/steamcondenser.json` while four blocktypes still named it -
`Every_shape_reference_resolves_to_a_shipped_file` was red in the working tree. Restored. The editable copy
still carries `game:`-domained texture paths, which Model Creator cannot resolve, so it wants un-domaining
before anyone opens it.

⛔ **The 5 x 5 grid still has no shipped instance.** Measured across every recipe in the tree: the largest
`Quantity(n)` anywhere is **8**, once, and every machine recipe is `.Size(3, 3)` or smaller. The bench
ships at 5 x 5 because widening or narrowing costs nothing either way (the matcher is size-agnostic and the
window is a loop over slot count), but workbench.md's *"size the grid against a real bill of materials
first"* is unanswered until the boiler or the engine is written. The handbook cost it names is unpaid too:
nothing renders a wider-than-3 recipe.

**Not built:** the interaction sequence. The design makes it opt-in - no declared sequence is a
one-interaction craft - so the bench is complete without it, and `config/craftsequences/` does not exist
yet.

## 2026-08-15 - The workbench is designed; two small forming rulings land

**Design only, nothing built.** Settled with the owner in one pass and written up as
[workbench.md](../../design/machines/workbench.md), with a dated amendment in
[diagram-crafting.md](../../design/mechanics/diagram-crafting.md) so a reader meets the exception where
Model A is read rather than only in the page that introduced it.

⛔⛔ **A 5 x 5 bench is justified by STACK SIZE, and my first answer against it was wrong.** I argued the
grid was already a bill of materials and nine distinct ingredients is plenty - which quietly assumed a cell
holds the whole quantity. It does not: a `GridRecipe` ingredient's `quantity` is bounded by what the slot
holds, so **48 rods against a 16 cap is unsatisfiable in one cell** however the recipe is written. Three
cells of rods is not a picture of the product, so Model A's "one cell per distinct ingredient" is untouched.

★★ **No new recipe engine, and that was worth checking before answering.** `GridRecipe.Width`/`.Height` are
plain settable properties defaulting to 3, and `ConsumeInput` takes an arbitrary `gridWidth` and scans
sub-positions - so a 5 x 5 bench reuses vanilla's matcher whole and every existing 3 x 3 recipe works in it
for free. Model A's clause survives; what is new is the **interaction sequence**.

★ The owner's rules, recorded as rules: no declared sequence means a one-interaction craft (the tedium
guard); repeat-while-held applies to **single-step** recipes only; a wrong gesture does nothing; output is
an inventory, because the stack cap that motivates the input applies on the way out too - which is also
what frees the empty hand to be a sequence step.

★ Everything is JSON: recipes are ordinary grid recipes up to 5 wide, sequences live in
`config/craftsequences/` keyed by machine and merged like the crop tables, and a step's `tool` reuses
`MachineTool`'s tier rather than inventing a second hardness idea. A third party ships a bench and its
labour without C#.

### Two forming rulings

- **The shear cuts a vanilla plate into 2 nail plates.** A **whole-item** job - a vanilla plate carries no
  work piece, so the row has no stage and no family, which `ShearFeed` already handles. 200 u to 2 x 100.
- ⛔ **The 500 u `castplate` is struck** (owner): it was never a second item, only the old name of
  `castplate-heavy`. D2 records the correction and rolled-parts.md's "heavyplate is not castplate" section
  is rewritten as *the rolled and the cast plate are one item* - same 600 u, differing only in metal, which
  the redrawn cast art now carries (10 x 2 x 12 = 240 vx3, the rolled one's volume exactly).

⛔ **Recipe interchangeability is deferred** to the recipe pass (owner). M.8 is otherwise unblocked: one
item, a metal variant, `castplate-heavy` re-massed 160 -> 600 with its pattern capacity following.

## 2026-08-15 - Cast iron stops laundering into vanilla iron; stage ladders become process routes

Five things, all owner-directed, all in the forming/materials seam. Gate is 9 targets / **4,251**.

### Cast and pig iron pay out in their own bits

⛔⛔ **`castiron.json` generated `iiex:metalbit-castiron`, shipped its lang - and named `game:metalbit-iron`
as the thing a chisel actually recovers.** So the item existed, was obtainable nowhere, and every shattered
mold, chiselled canal and broken hearth-metal block paid out in **vanilla** iron. Twenty of those smelt to a
plain iron ingot, which is forgeable: cast iron off a cupola became anything wrought iron could be, and
puddling was optional. `pigiron.json` had the same line and no bits form at all, so the cheapest metal in the
game leaked the same way.

★ **The source had predicted the fix in place**: *"giving either metal its own solid drop in the registry
changes the result with no edit here"* (`BlockHearthMetal.cs:52`). One field each.

⛔ **Closing a leak can open a dead end, and nearly did.** The cupola charges only items holding the `scrap`
role, and neither new bit was in that list - so the metal a player recovered would have been metal nothing
accepts. Both added, and `ShippedMetalDropTests` now holds all three halves: a metal that generates its own
bits must drop them, no metal may pay out in a vanilla bit, and whatever it pays out must be chargeable back.

⛔ **Two shipped tests had pinned the leak as intentional** - `SolidDropOf_castiron_yields_the_shared_vanilla_scrap`
and `Pig_iron_is_an_iwex_feedstock_dropping_shared_vanilla_scrap`, both citing "mod alloys shatter to vanilla
bits as scrap". The `castiron` one even noted that `metalbit-castiron` "still exists, but is not what a broken
casting sheds" - the inconsistency was seen and rationalised rather than followed.

★ **Still open, same shape**: `bessemersteel.json` drops `game:metalbit-steel`, which is the blown-iron
ruling's own gate (N1) leaking the same way. It ships no bits form, so fixing it is a content decision rather
than a field, and N1 is already recorded as contradicted by shipped data.

### `StageLadder` is `ProcessRoute`

Owner's naming call. `ProcessRoute` / `ProcessRouteLoader` / `ProcessRouteRegistry`, attribute key
`processroute`, catalogue `config/processroutes/`, so the whole extension surface reads as one family beside
`ProcessStage`, `ProcessJob` and `ProcessJobRegistry`. Checked against `dist/Releases/` first: exlib 0.7.2
carries none of these types, so no published contract broke and no legacy key was owed.

⛔ **A blanket word sweep is not a rename.** "Ladder" is also the design's word for the blade **temper**
ladder and the tier ladder, and five call sites came back reading "tier route". Swept back by hand.

### The half-step is a stage, and it is not a rung

★★ **A gap is taken in two rounds, so between two settings there is a real state with real geometry** - and
the rod and bar art have drawn it all along (`Grooved175`, `Flattened225`, and six more). Nothing could
declare it: a stage a family accepts becomes a **gap band on the deck**, so declaring 1.75 would have doubled
the mill's controls and made the two-round model a four-gap one.

So `"halfStep": true`, and the two readings split - `RungsFor(family)` for the machine's settings, `StageAt`
for the renderer. ⛔ A half-step may not name a `code`; the parser refuses one, because a product declared
where the player cannot stop reads as reachable and is obtainable nowhere.

★ **`ShippedProcessRouteTests` is the guard the drawn-art route never had**: every declared element must
exist in the shape its route names (a typo is silent - it falls back to the composed mesh), and every
half-step must be the **exact midpoint** of a gap its branch takes.

⛔⛔ **That guard caught my own error on its first run.** I bounded half-steps by the rungs, and all four
shipped ones failed: the walk starts at the form's **entry** thickness, which is not a rung - stock arrives
at it rather than being set to it - so the topmost half-step is above every rung by construction.

### The bar's route ends where its products begin *(owner rulings)*

- **The grooved branch stops at the cut.** At grooved 2.0 a bar is 40.5 long; the player goes to the shear
  and cuts four rods, and only then can the metal be rolled on (each rod re-enters as `stock-rod`). The
  1.5 and 1.0 grooved rungs are gone - they were art-less and unreachable in play.
- ★★ **The beam is one item, claimed at the mill, and its own form.** A bar taken flat to 2.0 *is* a beam:
  4.5 × 2 × 18, the bar's whole 162 vx³, so `BeamUnits` went **200 → 400** and one bar makes one beam with
  no shear. Everything the bar's flat branch had below 2.0 moved into `processroutes/beam.json`, and the
  beam re-enters the mill as `iiex:stock-beam` through the same feedstock table the rod uses. The plate crop
  moved with it: two plates come off a **beam** at 1.0, not off a bar.
- **The wide rolls have their own family.** `flat` and `flatwide` shared one, so a route could not say "wide
  only" - it was implied by the narrow set's `accepts` and nothing else. The slab and the two cast wide forms
  now declare `flatwide`; the bar declares both, since it runs on either barrel today.

⛔ **Two consequences to settle.** The cast billet's beam crop row is **removed**: 600 u of billet against a
400 u beam does not divide, and a declared yield that cannot be paid is exactly what these guards exist to
stop. And the **shingled slab cannot be given the same treatment** - only its base is drawn
(`item-shingled-slab.json` has one element), so it has no stage art, no half-steps and no drawn product.

### The wide route, once its art arrived *(owner drew it)*

`item-shingled-slab.json` gained its stages and `item-rolled-heavyplate.json` arrived, so the wide side got
the same treatment the narrow one just had - and the art settled two numbers rather than asking for them.

★★ **The drawn slab spreads at e = 1.0, not the 0.463 that shipped.** Read off four stages at once: 8 wide
at 3.0 becoming 8.7 / 9.6 / 10.6 / 12 at 2.75 / 2.5 / 2.25 / 2.0. The old exponent was a guess from before
anyone drew it, and every generated slab stage was regenerated against the new one.

★★ **The wide chain is bar-and-beam again, one tier up.** A slab's route ends at 2.0, where the art draws
`CutHeavyPlate1` - 12 × 2 × 20, which is **two** heavy plates side by side - so the shear takes two, and each
re-enters the mill as `iiex:stock-heavyplate` on its own route. That route ends where the art draws
`Boilerplate`, 15 × 1 × 16, claimed at the mill with no cut. Every mass falls out exactly: 1200 = 2 × 600,
and 600 in is 600 out.

⛔ **This ships a rolled `iiex:heavyplate`, which M.8 said not to do** - that stage exists to merge it with
`castplate-heavy` onto one metal axis, and there are now two items where M.8 wants one. Shipped anyway
because the route is drawn and the slab dead-ends without it; M.8 now has two items to merge rather than
one, which is the cost that ruling named.

### Every generated stage shape is gone; the drawn art is the only art

★★ **All 39 went, not 31.** The first pass kept one base file per form on the grounds that
`StockItemDefinitions` points an item at it and `ItemStockPiece` scales it. Both are still true - but the
base state is *already drawn*, as `ShingledBar1` / `RolledRod200` / `Beam` / `HeavyPlate1` / `ShingledSlab1`
inside the family shape its route names. So a form declares `Shape` + `BaseElement` and the item renders
that element with `selectiveElements`, which keeps the composed mesh to the one element too. No generated
copy of a hand-drawn state.

⛔ **And the three cast base shapes were read by nothing even before that.** `stock-cast{billet,bloom,slab}`
sat in `assets/iiex/shapes/forming/` while the pieces they belonged to render from `iiex:item/cast{form}` -
the cast three mint no stock item, so nothing ever resolved those paths. They were art in the wrong mod's
folder for a form that does not draw stages.

### The guard moved off generated art

★★ **The guard moved from generated art to authored art, which is the version that can disagree.**
Measuring generated stages against the spread model is close to tautological - the generator implements the
model. `RolledStockStagesTests` now measures the **elements a route draws**, and that check is what showed
the shingled slab was drawn at e = 1 while its form declared 0.463.

⛔ **Its tolerance is 10%, not a decimal place, and the reason is worth keeping.** Authored art is drawn to
settled product sections and the model only approximates them - a beam is 4.5 wide because that is what a
beam is, where the exponent says 4.23. The widest shipped disagreement is 6%; a wrong exponent was 24%.

⛔ **Grooved stages are excluded on purpose.** A groove constrains the spread rather than letting it run
sideways, so grooved art is drawn square while the form spreads - `Grooved250` is 2.5 wide against a model
saying 3.5. One exponent per form cannot express both, and a guard over it would assert something the
design does not claim. Recorded rather than papered over.

### Mod-gated material roles are data now

`MaterialRoleDef.RequiresMod` gates a row on another mod being loaded, so the six IndustrialStory / Expanded
Matter ore codes moved out of `IronOreCompat.Contribute` and into `materialroles.json`. Supporting another
mod's ore is a JSON row rather than a branch in our C#, which is what E1-E4 asks for. The code-contributor
hook stays for anything genuinely dynamic; it now has no shipped user.

★ **`convert-shape.py --merge`** joins several editables into one runtime shape, because a route names a
single `shape` and the bar's stages are authored across two files. ⛔ Its clash test compares **geometry**,
not the element: the same piece re-drawn in a second file keeps its box and gets new UVs, and a byte
comparison called the legitimate join a conflict.

## 2026-08-15 - The 25 u blank is a RIVET ROD, and the name was hiding a quarter-scale item

**Owner ruling, prompted by exactly the right question: "why does a vanilla rod become 4 of our rods?"**
It does not. `game:rod-iron` is 2 × 2 × 10 at 100 u; the product is 1 × 1 × 10 at **25 u** - a quarter of
the section and a quarter of the mass - and the metal conserves exactly (100 u rolled grooved to 1.0 is
1 × 1 × 40, cut into four 10s). The count of 4 is geometry, not a yield. ⛔ But the *name* was `rod`, so
the handbook and the crop table showed "Iron Rod" and "Rod" side by side with nothing to tell a player that
one weighs a quarter of the other. Renamed to **`iiex:rivetrod`** / "Rivet Rod", named for the bench that
cuts and upsets it. Gate is 9 targets / **4,192**.

★★ **The design had already ruled the other way, and the ruling that overwrote it did not know it was
doing so.** `fasteners.md` gotcha 4 struck `rivetrod` as "a dead name ... the bench is a heading machine,
not a rivet machine" - but `STATE.md`'s settled **Fasteners** row of 2026-07-30 says the opposite in as
many words: *"two machines, two routes, no dies, no bolts ... the rivet machine cuts and upsets rod @
25 u"*. `heading-machine.md` had introduced the `ItemDie` contract and a bolt route on top of that row
without recording a supersession, so a settled decision was replaced by a page nobody read against it.
The owner's ruling restores the original.

⛔ **Only the item was renamed; the bench is now an open question rather than a silent contradiction.**
`heading-machine.md` still owns the `ItemDie` contract that `machining-line.md` cites, so re-settling it is
not a local edit and nothing there is built. Recorded on that page, on `fasteners.md` gotcha 4 and on the
`STATE.md` row, so the next reader meets the conflict instead of picking whichever page they opened first.

★ **`StockForm.Rod` was NOT renamed and should not be.** The 100 u work piece a rod becomes on entering the
mill is a rod - full section, being rolled. It is the *product* of the grooved branch that is a quarter of
one. Two different things, and only one of them was misnamed.

★ The item's art was already right and had been the tell all along: the shape file is
`iiex:item/rolled-rivetrod` and its elements are `CutRivetRod1…4`.

Touched: `RolledItemDefinitions.cs`, `assets/iiex/config/processjobs/shear.json`, three lang files, the
`rod.json` golden renamed to `rivetrod.json`, `rolled-parts.md`, `fasteners.md`, `heading-machine.md`,
`nail-machine.md`, `STATE.md`, `NEXT.md`.

## 2026-08-14 - The rod fork: the mill now takes something it did not make

**A vanilla `game:rod-iron` can be fed to the mill.** Down the grooved branch it becomes 4 × `iiex:rod`
@ 25 u at the shear; down the flat one it becomes 1 × `iiex:nailplate` with no crop at all. Both fastener
benches - the nail machine and the heading machine - now have an obtainable input, and every one of the six
rolled products is reachable. Gate is 9 targets / **4,192**.

★★ **Admission is the new seam, and it is keyed on CODES rather than on forms.**
`StockForm.RegisterFeedstock(offered, entersAs)` is a table of *offered item code → the stock item it
enters as*, applied by `BlockEntityRollingMill.Admit` with the heat carried across. That is what lets the
re-rollable rod stay vanilla's: 32-odd call sites across the mods ask for `game:rod-*` by name, and minting
ours would have split every consumer in two. Keying on codes rather than forms means a caller admitting
their own feedstock never has to know a `StockForm` exists.

⛔ **It has to run before the deck's gap mapping, not just before the feed.** `BlockRollingMill.Feed`
resolves the schedule off the offered piece to decide how many gap bands the deck has; a rod admitted only
inside `TryFeed` would have picked its band against a schedule of one and then been fed at another. So
`Admit` is called in both places and is idempotent - a piece already being rolled comes back by reference,
which is also what stops a second conversion discarding the gauge it had reached.

★ **The rod is the one family drawn at every rung**, so its ladder names a `shape` and a per-stage
`element` and `ItemStockPiece` renders `Grooved150` / `Flattened150` / `NailPlate` as authored. Every other
form composes its mesh by scaling the base, which is right for a rectangle and wrong for a groove.

★ **Its flat 1.0 rung is the design's one whole-piece conversion**, and the ladder expresses it without a
crop row: the stage names `code: iiex:nailplate` with `generate: false`, so the mill claims it on the way
out and `ProcessItemEmitter` builds nothing (the item is hand-authored, and a generated duplicate would be
rejected by the object loader).

⛔ **The blocker recorded against this was real.** `RolledStockStagesTests` iterated a fixed
`[3, 2, 1.5, 1, 0.5]`, which assumes every form leaves the helve 3 thick - so a rod entering at 2.0 would
have demanded a `stock-rod-30`, art for a state the piece can never be in. The list is per-form now in both
suites, mirroring `generate-rolled-stock.py`'s own `stages_for`.

★ `StockFormRegistryTests`' seed check asserted `Assert.Equal(2, StockForm.All.Count)`, which every new
form breaks for no reason worth reading. It asserts the whole set now, so a seed that forgets a form and a
seed that gains one read differently.

Mutation-checked: dropping the `RegisterFeedstock` row fails both admission tests, at the registry and at
the station.

Touched: `StockForm.cs`, `StockItemDefinitions.cs`, `RollSetItemDefinitions.cs`,
`BlockEntityRollingMill.cs`, `BlockRollingMill.cs`, `generate-rolled-stock.py`, four generated stage shapes
+ `assets/iiex/shapes/forming/rod.json`, `assets/iiex/config/stageladders/rod.json`,
`assets/iiex/config/processjobs/shear.json`, three lang files, two goldens, `RollingMillStationTests.cs`,
`StockFormRegistryTests.cs`, `RolledStockStagesTests.cs`, `CastStockFormsTests.cs`,
`ShippedCropTableTests.cs`, `ShippedCastCropTableTests.cs`, and the docs that own any of it.

## 2026-08-14 - Cast stock rolls: the forms landed, and the piece had been there all along

**A billet, a bloom and a slab now go through the mill and come off the shear as products.** Five crop
rows work — billet to 3 beams or 3 plates, bloom to 5 blanks or 5 skelps, slab to 5 boiler plates — which
takes the shipped crop table from four rows to nine. Gate is 9 targets / **4,148**.

★★ **The unit was specified as "add three items" and needed none.** `iiex:caststock-{billet,bloom,slab}`
has shipped since U1 at exactly the settled masses (600 / 1000 / 3000), poured by the long cell's three
lane patterns. What did not exist was anything telling the mill what those pieces *were*. So the itemtype
gained a `stockForm` attribute and `ItemStockPiece` as its class, siex registered the three `StockForm`s,
and the stage art stayed where it was generated. **iiex ships the pieces, siex makes them rollable** — an
iiex-only player holds cast stock the rolls refuse, which is the tier gate falling out of the mod split
rather than being built.

⛔ **The reheat hearth had not recognised a single cast piece since that U1 rename.**
`HeatingHearthLayout.StockOf` still tested the prefixes `castbillet` / `castbloom` / `castslab` against an
item that had become `caststock-{form}`, so `StockOf` returned null for all three — on the one tier that
is on the crosswise seating from its first pass, i.e. the tier that most needs reheating. The rows pinning
it were three literals, which is why nothing was red; they read `CastStockItemDefinitions.Forms` now.

⛔⛔ **`StockItemDefinitions` emitted one item per REGISTERED form, and the registry is shared.** The doc
comment sold that as a feature — "the item list and `StockForm.All` cannot diverge" — but `All` is a table
any mod may add to, so the first foreign form would have minted an `iiex:stock-<theirs>` for somebody
else's stock and then thrown `KeyNotFoundException` on the mass lookup. It emits the forms it holds a mass
for now. The three cast forms would have tripped it on the same day they were added.

⛔⛔ **`bloom` is the shingled bar's former name, and a former name resolves.** A cast bloom declaring the
bare variant as its form would have been rolled as a **400 u wrought bar** with the mass as the only
symptom. Hence `FormOf`, which writes `cast` + variant, and a guard that asserts the collision rather than
the fix — the wrought lookup still resolving is the premise.

★★ **The wide roll sets are cancelled, not deferred** *(owner ruling)*. steel-roll-sets.md proposed
`rollset-flatwide35` and `-flatwide30` as smex items so 4-thick cast stock could be bitten. It should not:
the wide stand's top roller is the movable one and the player sets the gap on the mill itself
([machines.txt](../workbench/machines.txt)'s `i1` cells), so **one wide set covers every wide gap** and
3.5 / 3.0 are ordinary rungs on the two cast ladders. ⛔ The cost is exactly what that page's own open
question warned of: one item carries one `MinTorque`, so the wide route has **no torque gate at all** and
cast stock rolls behind the iron-tier 0.5. The shear side does gate — every cast crop asks `minTier: 2`,
the steel blade.

★ **The billet's grooved branch is declared nowhere, on purpose.** Carried to the grooved 2.0 gap a billet
is 60.75 long, past the hearth in both seatings; the route wants the mid-gap crop at the 2.25 half-step,
and that crop yields six pieces each still needing a pass — which is not the one-product-plus-remainder
stroke the shear does, and is refused outright by `FeedVerdict.PartCropped`. The bloom's wide 3.0 crop is
the same question. Both are now one open item rather than two, and it is the highest-value forming ruling
left: it also closes B12, which this unit turned from a carried question into a measured one (a bloom
rolled to 1.0 is 8 × 1 × **50** against the 48 seating — a cost, not a lock, since the shear cuts cold).

★ **`ShapeExtents` in exlib's test library, and both stock-art guards now use it.** A child element's
