## 2026-08-12 - the backlog was half stale, and the audit did not know we ship three versions

Went at the § B teardown cluster and found **five of the first seven rows already fixed** by the A0–A3
work and never struck off. Swept the rest rather than keep discovering it one row at a time: **11 of 30
were dead**. § B is now 17 done / 13 open and the header says so. The rule the file already carried -
open the file before acting on a row - turns out to apply to its own bookkeeping.

⛔⛔ **The audit was written against the 1.22 source alone, and we ship 1.20/1.21/1.22.** Its fix for the
container protocol names `CachedAccessPerms` and `SendInventoryRollback`; a byte scan of the three
`VintagestoryAPI.dll`s says **both are 1.22-only**. Applied as written it would have broken ten of the
fifteen targets. Now recorded at the top of § B: check any recommended member against `.game/1.20` and
`.game/1.21` first.

**Two rows were genuinely live and are fixed.**

`RecalculateAndSyncOrientations` wrote `beNet.Orientation = Orientation` - `this`, the **initiator**.
Correct on the one call from `OnBlockPlaced`, wrong on the six from `Rotate`, which run it against each
neighbour of the block being wrenched: every one of those neighbours got the wrenched block's
orientation persisted and synced. Every other read in the method already went through `netBlock`.
`OrientationSyncTests` pins both cases, and the second (initiator == target) is what stops the fix being
indistinguishable from writing nothing.

**The machine station's packet protocol** gained the three things vanilla's container handler does and
ours did not: the interaction-range test, an inventory rollback on refusal, and
`GetChunkAtBlockPos(Pos)?.MarkModified()`. It landed in **exlib** rather than the design table because
A3 moved the handshake there, so every machine gets it. Without the range test a client could move slots
in any unclaimed station in any loaded chunk from arbitrary distance; without MarkModified a slot move
not followed by another write is lost on the next restart.

★★ The range test is behind `GAME_GE_1_22` **and** an `internal virtual ValidatePickRange`. The seam
exists because a substitute `IPlayer` is never in range of anything - the engine's block-position
`IsInInteractionRangeOf` is internal, so the mock returns false and *every* packet route was rejected.
The claim check is deliberately **not** behind it: turning off the range test still leaves the rejection
tests testing rejection. `internal`, so no third-party mod can reach it; exlib grants
`InternalsVisibleTo("IronworkingExpanded.Tests")` for the double.

**The JSON patch cluster**, three rows: 27 `refractory.json` entries and `disable-crushing.json` gained
`"side": "Server"` (the default is Universal, not the target file's category, so they were being
evaluated client-side and logging a miss each); `crushed-fettlestock.json` and `metalbit.json` went from
`op: add` to `addmerge`, which the adapter's `default:` branch shows is safe on a key vanilla does not
have yet - it assigns when absent and merges when present. `disable-crushing.json` keeps its positional
`/2` index (the patch system has no key-based array addressing) but now names EM **3.7.0** and what
index 2 actually is: coke to 4x crushed-ore-coal, a hand-hammer fuel multiplier the coke oven cannot
survive.

New guard `Every_patch_entry_declares_the_side_it_runs_on` over all 37 shipped patch entries, with a
non-empty corpus assertion beside it. Mutation-checked, as were the other two fixes.

## 2026-08-12 - the shape/mesh seam closed, and a shared face that wrote back into the asset

Six § B rows, all one seam: how a shape gets off disk and into a mesh. 15/15 green
(exlib 1656, iwex 1600, lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243).

⛔⛔ **`ExShapeElements.Retextured` was repointing the source asset.** `Shape.Clone()` deep-copies the
element tree, but `ShapeElement.Clone()` copies faces with `(ShapeElementFace[])FacesResolved?.Clone()`
(`.compat/.../ShapeElement.cs:369`) - an **array** clone whose entries are the **same objects**. Writing
`face.Texture` on the "copy" therefore rewrote the shape every other block draws from. Harmless while
every caller fed it a freshly deserialised shape; a live cross-block corruption the moment shapes are
cached, which is exactly what this pass does. A repointed face is now a fresh `ShapeElementFace` written
into the cloned array slot. The array slot was always ours; the face behind it never was.

⛔ **`BEBehaviorMPSubmachineBase` was rewriting its blocktype's shape path.** `WithPathPrefixOnce` mutates
the receiver and returns it, so the missing `.Clone()` permanently rewrote `Block.Shape.Base` for every
instance - and since `GetShape()` builds from it, the CompositeShape's hash changed after first
tesselation, which is what `MechNetworkRenderer.AddDevice` pools renderers on. The device migrated to a
second bucket with its own uploaded mesh. The per-behaviour `_baseMesh` field and `ResetBaseMesh` are
gone with it: the body mesh is a pure function of the blocktype, the engine snaps a generator with
`ExchangeBlock`, and a different code simply misses the cache entry rather than needing a hand-off.

**Mesh cache adoption finished** - both hearths, the sand-casting cell, the reinforced hopper and the MP
body. Keyed the way vanilla's firepit keys (`BEFirepit.getOrCreateMesh`): the hearths on what is lying on
the bed, the casting cell on the shape reference its state already reduces to. ★★ Every `Rotate`/
`Translate` happens **inside the factory** or on a `Clone` - a cached mesh is shared, and the hopper's
contents pile would otherwise climb a little higher in every hopper on every tesselation.

**All ten remaining raw `Assets.TryGet(...).ToObject<Shape>()` loads** now go through
`ExMeshCache.LoadShape` (`Shape.TryGet`), which catches, names the file and sets
`ShapeElement.locationForLogging`. The four animator sites are deliberately **not** cached and say so:
`InitializeAnimator` resolves joints into the shape it is handed, so each animator needs its own.

★★ **Two source guards** (`ShapeLoadingGuards`) - no raw `ToObject<Shape>`, no in-place `Shape.Base
.WithPath`. They match **whole-file**, not line-by-line, because csharpier breaks a fluent chain across
lines and a per-line scan would miss exactly the spellings the formatter produces. Both mutation-checked,
and the corpus asserts non-empty so neither can pass by scanning nothing.

**`PossibleOrientations` off `System.Text.Json`** onto the tree's own `StringArrayAttribute`, via new
`ExTree.SetStrings`/`GetStrings`. Same key: `GetStringArray` safe-casts, so an old JSON-string value
reads as absent rather than throwing, and the old encoding is simply the second thing tried. ⛔ Without
that fallback every world already on disk loses its wrench rotation choices silently - `SaveFormatTests`
already carried a test saying so, and it goes red if the fallback is removed. `GetStrings` returns
**null** rather than `[]` when absent, which is what makes "never written" distinguishable from "written
empty"; that distinction is the whole migration. The smoke stack's private copy of the pair went too.

Not verifiable headless: the suite cannot tesselate, so green here means the keys and the copy semantics
are right, not that anything renders correctly. Wants a look in-world.

## 2026-08-12 - the mill claims a finished item when no shear is needed, and the stale codes are gone

Owner ruling, which corrected my earlier read: **the mill ejects one piece; if that piece is final it
throws the final item, otherwise it throws stock for the shear.** A conversion changes what the piece
is, never how many there are - counts belong to the shear.

**Grooved is `[2.5, 2.0, 1.5, 1.0]`** (owner's numbers; the groove bottoms at 1.0 and cannot close
further). Every step is a 0.5 draft, comfortably inside δ_max 1.0. This replaces yesterday's
`[2.0, 1.0, 0.5]`, which was legal but invented. Flat and flatwide were already correct and are untouched.

**All four stale output codes deleted, not re-pointed.** `iwex:rolledplate-iron`,
`iwex:rolledsheet-iron`, `iwex:wirerod-iron`, `iwex:nailrod-iron` named no item that exists, and two
contradict settled rulings: D7 puts wire in **elex**, and `rolled-parts.md` states "nails come from
plate, never from rod". `game:rod-iron` went too - it is real, but the crop table makes it a shear
product, 4 per `rolledrod`, not a mill output.

`ClaimFinishedPiece` wires `OutputAt` into `CompletePass`: on an **even** piece whose stage names an
output, the stack is swapped for that item and **the heat carries across**, so a piece claimed straight
off the rolls is still workable. An uneven piece is never claimed - it has passes left, and claiming
there would let a player stop half way and keep the whole item. A code resolving to no item logs and
leaves the piece as stock rather than destroying it.

⛔ **`RollSetSpec.TryParse` required at least one output** - "a set with no named stopping point makes
nothing". That was true under the old model and is now wrong: every shipped stage is a shear crop, so
every set legitimately declares `outputs: []`. Relaxed, and the test that pinned the old rule now states
the new one.

★★ **The catalogue cannot be expressed on the current key.** `Outputs` is keyed on **gap alone**, but
flat 1.0 yields `nailplate` from a `rolledrod` and `game:metalplate` from a bloom - same gap, different
product, decided by the form that entered. The one genuine whole-piece conversion in the whole design is
therefore unwritable until the key becomes **(form, gap)**. Recorded rather than worked around.

15/15 green: exlib 1649, iwex 1599, lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-12 - a shared mesh cache, and the argument that was never a cache key

First unit of the vanilla-practice half of the architecture pass, and it turns on one API misreading.

⛔⛔ **`ITesselatorAPI.TesselateShape`'s first argument is `typeForLogging`** - the interface documents it
"used for error logging only", and vanilla's `BECoalPile` passes the literal `"coalpile"` for eight
differently-sized shapes. Our charge pile built `"chargepile-" + element` for it under a comment
beginning *"The cache key must name every input to the mesh"*. The discipline in that comment is
correct and was being applied to a parameter that caches nothing, so the block re-read its shape JSON,
deep-cloned it and re-tesselated on every chunk re-tesselation, on the tesselation thread. Four block
entities did this with no caching of any kind: firebox, heating hearth, puddling hearth, charge pile.

`ExMeshCache` (exlib) wraps vanilla's own idiom - `ObjectCacheUtil.GetOrCreate` over `api.ObjectCache`,
which is what `BEIngotMold` and `BEGenericTypedContainer` use - under one `exlib:mesh:` prefix. The
block-code overload folds `Block.Code` into the key for the caller, because the failure it prevents
renders one block as another and reads as an art bug rather than a caching one. `LoadShape` goes through
`Shape.TryGet`, which catches and attributes a deserialisation failure instead of throwing it on the
tesselation thread; our own `docs/internal/vanilla/practices.md` had already ruled that and no call site followed it.

⛔ **`BlockEntityMoltenCanal` had a `_baseMesh` field and no null guard**, so it looked cached and was
assigned on every call. It is the same defect wearing a disguise.

⛔⛔ **Two aliasing traps a naive cache would have introduced, both now guarded in comments.** The canal
rotates its mesh, and `Rotate` mutates in place - rotating a shared mesh per call compounds. The charge
pile `Scale`s and `Translate`s per band, so it must `Clone()` the cached mesh before shaping it, or one
pile's bands drag every other pile's with them. The cache holds the pre-transform mesh in both cases.

Adopted on the firebox, the charge pile and the canal. Not yet on the heating and puddling hearths, or
the sand-casting cell.

⚠ **The cache is client-side and the headless suite cannot tesselate**, so a green run does not
demonstrate it works in game. What is tested is what decides correctness rather than speed: build-once
per key, no collision between blocktypes sharing a variant key, invalidation, and that `ShapePathOf`
does not mutate the shared `Block.Shape` (`WithPathPrefixOnce` mutates in place). Removing the
block-code folding turns the collision test red.

15/15 green: exlib 1649, iwex 1595, lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-12 - the mill can be fed, reached and walked; the products are not its job

B4 and B17 closed, and B3b re-read against the design rather than implemented.

**B4 — the rod route had no legal entry.** `grooved` shipped gaps `[1.0, 0.5]`, and δ_max is μ²R =
0.5² × 4 = **1.0**, so 3.0 bloom onto a 1.0 gap was a 2.0 draft that could never bite. Now
`[2.0, 1.0, 0.5]`; both named stopping points unchanged, so the outputs need no edit.

⛔ **A golden pinned those numbers and could not catch this.** A golden says the data *changed*, not
that it is *wrong* — regenerating it accepts a broken ladder silently. `ShippedRollSetTests` now checks
the corpus behaviourally: every shipped set must be walkable from its accepted form's fresh thickness,
every rung a legal bite. Reverting the fix under it prints `grooved on bloom: 3 -> 1 is a draft of 2,
over delta_max 1`.

**B17 — worse than recorded.** `IsInputDeck` compared against one cell while `MillFeed.AlongBarrel`
maps a click across all three of the footprint's deck cells, so the reachable span was `[0.667, 1.0)` —
the last third of the barrel. The note said "zones 2-3 of 4"; in fact for **any** gap count above one
the widest gap was unreachable, and the widest gap is the only one fresh stock can enter at. So the
mill could not take a first bite from a real click on any multi-gap set. `IsInputDeck` now accepts the
whole row; `Deck()` still returns the single cell a finished piece lands on.

★★ **Three tests covered this area and none could see it.** `MillFeedTests` exercises `AlongBarrel`
over the full `localX ∈ [-2, 0]` domain — a value the block can never produce — and `RollingMillFeedTests`
calls `TryFeed(stack, gapIndex, strip)` directly, bypassing the click path entirely. Both pass either
way. The new tests walk the row and assert every gap zone is reachable.

**B3b — not implemented, on purpose.** `RollSetSpec.OutputAt` has no production caller and four of the
five codes the sets name exist nowhere. The obvious fix is to call it from `CompletePass` — and the
settled design says not to: `rolling-mill.md` Open #1 and `roll-sets.md` put `Outputs`/`OutputAt` on a
future **shear station**, keyed on stage rather than gap, because "the mill only ever makes stock;
there is no claim gesture on the mill", and Open #4 says to delete the four dangling codes. So the mill
is closed as *mechanically complete* — feed, reach, walk — and the product catalogue is a separate
unbuilt machine. Deleting the dangling codes is left as a content call.

Also found and pinned, not fixed: `slitting` accepts only `plate`, which is not a `StockForm`, so it
can never take anything. `flat` and `grooved` also list `billet`, equally unreal. The dead-set list is
asserted as exactly `["slitting"]` so a second one cannot appear unnoticed.

15/15 green: exlib 1644, iwex 1595, lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-12 - a block entity declares its state once, and both directions follow

A4, built for the reason I had undervalued: exlib is a **library**, and the ergonomics of its API are
a deliverable in their own right, not only its defect count. A hand-written
`ToTreeAttributes`/`FromTreeAttributes` pair spells every field twice, and a field added to one side
and forgotten on the other still compiles - it saves and never loads, or loads and never reaches the
client.

`ExBlockState` holds the declarations; `ExBlockEntity` is a base that owns one and needs no wiring.
**Composable on purpose**: a block entity whose base slot is already spent - a container, a multiblock,
a network node - owns an `ExBlockState` directly and calls it from its own two overrides. Building it
only as a base class would have repeated exactly the mistake A3 just undid.

Kinds: bool / int / long / float / double / string / enum / `BlockPos` / `ItemStack`. Three of those
carry judgement rather than plumbing. A **null reference field round-trips as null**, so an absent
`BlockPos` does not come back as the origin - a real cell that would silently re-anchor whatever reads
it. An **enum persists as its int**, so renaming a member does not move saved data. And a declared
**stack carries its collectible id mappings both ways**, which is what makes a block entity survive a
schematic paste: `ItemStack.ToBytes` writes the runtime id, and a stack the destination cannot map is
**dropped rather than kept**, matching vanilla's `BlockEntityIngotMold`. Nine of the eleven
stack-persisting block entities in the repo write that by hand today; this makes it free.

Declaring one key twice **throws at declaration**. It is the copy-paste slip the type exists to
prevent - the second field silently overwrites the first on save and both load the same value - and it
cannot be caught by reading the code, because it compiles and runs.

Adopted on `BlockEntityRollingMill`, which proves the composable half: its base is spent on being a
station, so it owns the state object. Seven hand-written field pairs became seven declarations. Two
mutations - dropping the failed-mapping arm, and returning origin instead of null for an absent
position - each turn a test red.

15/15 green: exlib 1644 (+7), iwex 1589, lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-11 - the rolling mill could not roll anything, and A4 has mostly evaporated

⛔⛔ **`WorkPiece.FromStack` read `stockForm` from the stack tree only.** `StockItemDefinitions`
declares it on the **item type** (`.Attribute("stockForm", form.Name)`), and a freshly crafted bloom
carries nothing on its stack, so every unrolled piece read as "not stock". `MillFeed.Decide` refused it
as `WrongForm`. **No player could roll anything at all** - the whole forming line was dead on arrival,
and it shipped that way. `FromStack` now falls back to the item type; the stack still wins once
something has rolled the piece, because the type only says what it started as.

★★ **Twenty passing feed tests could not see it.** Every one built its stock with
`WorkPiece.Fresh(...).ToStack(stack)` - hand-writing the state that production never writes before the
first pass. The fixture manufactured the precondition the code under test was supposed to establish.
This is [[fixture-stub-block-hides-production]] again, one level up: not a stub type, a stub *setup*.
Four new tests build stock the way the game does; mutating the fallback away turns both key ones red,
the mill-level one with `refused as WrongForm`.

**A4 was reconsidered and is largely already discharged**, so it was not built. Its four arms:
teardown symmetry is guarded by `TeardownSymmetryTests` (a text invariant, three facts); dialog
disposal moved into `BlockEntityMachineStation` in A3; renderer disposal is correct at every site
(the one file flagged is a partial-class artifact - the boiler registers in `.Client.cs` and disposes
in `.cs`); and the declared-synced-field arm has **zero live instances**. I scanned every
`GetBlockInfo` for private fields absent from `ToTreeAttributes`: three hits, all three false
positives (`_openingsCount` syncs under a different key name, `_clientFlowRate`/`_clientMaxVolume` are
client-side by construction, `_ironMeltingPoint`/`_meltStartDelay` are config-derived and identical on
both sides). A shared root refactoring 33 block entities would buy no defect reduction today. What A4
should become instead: the guard's **per-file** check has a partial-class blind spot, latent now but
one worth closing before more stations grow a `.Client.cs`.

15/15 green: exlib 1637, iwex 1588 (+3), lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-11 - the base slot goes to form, so a machine can be a container

Seam 3 of [framework composition](../../design/mechanics/framework-composition.md), plus the station
window promoted to exlib. A1 made membership a behaviour and then nothing adopted it:
`BlockEntityNetworkNode` still held the base slot of every machine that merely happened to be on a
network. A rolling mill is not a kind of network node, and while the slot was spent that way no such
machine could be a `BlockEntityContainer` - which is what a machine with a window is. That was the
real blocker under the machining line, not the process axis.

`BlockEntityMachineStation` (exlib) now carries the container, the open/close/slot packet handshake,
the claim check and the window disposal that every machine with a dialog was going to re-implement.
Slots are declared as `MachineSlotSpec` data and built through `InventoryGeneric`'s own slot-factory
delegate. `OnReceivedClientPacket` is **sealed** and machines extend `OnStationPacket` instead, so no
override can drop the claim check by forgetting `base` - the design table's did not, but it was one
edit away from it. `CreateDialog` returning null leaves a station windowless, which the mill is today.

Both consumers landed. `BlockEntityDesignTable` keeps only its slot rules and its draft; everything
else was boilerplate. `BlockEntityRollingMill` derives from the station and carries its membership and
its pass clock as behaviours, and its two loose `ItemStack` fields became inventory slots - which
retired its hand-written stack persistence **and** ~40 lines of collectible id mapping, the mapping
whose absence corrupts a schematic paste.

⛔ **A shipped machine's loose stacks need a load-time migration.** The mill has a grid recipe, so
`rmPiece`/`rmRollSet` are in published saves at the tree root; a container writes under `inventory`.
Without the one-way adopt, every saved mill comes back with its roll set gone and any piece mid-pass
destroyed, and nothing errors. Guarded three ways and each guard mutation-checked: disabling the adopt,
dropping its empty-slot precedence, and forgetting to host the membership each turn a test red.

Verified before touching anything, because each would have sunk the approach: `MpEnergyNetwork` resolves
consumers by `is IMpEnergyConsumer` and never by base class; the only two casts to the old base are
orientation and both degrade gracefully; the mill's orientation is a **block variant**, never the block
entity's `PossibleOrientations`. ⛔ And the filler's `PortFace`/`PortNetworkType` arm looked redundant
with hosted behaviours but is not - A1 deliberately kept it when it narrowed `INetworkConnector` to a
port. Left alone.

⛔ **A1's `NetworkSystem` setter trap, hit again.** A get-only read-through compiles and fails ~13
fixture sites that inject a test graph by reflection. The write-through `protected set` is load-bearing.

**Against vanilla's model, deliberately: ours stays.** `BlockMultiblock` needs a blocktype per offset
and puts no block entity on a filler cell at all; ours puts the offset in the filler's block entity,
which is the only reason a footprint cell can host a graph node or any other behaviour. Their recursion
guards are still worth borrowing.

Also: `scripts/test-floors.txt` is gone at the user's request, along with the per-suite floor plumbing
in `exmod.ps1`. The guard that mattered stays - a run whose summary is missing discovered no tests and
still exits 0, which is how a type-load failure reads as a blank PASS.

15/15 green: exlib 1637, iwex 1585 (+8), lpex 182, hpex 54, smex 243.

## 2026-08-11 - the production tick is a behaviour, and form no longer inherits process

Seam 1 of [framework composition](../../design/mechanics/framework-composition.md), the whole of it.
`BlockEntityMultiblockStructure` extended `BlockEntityProductionMachine`, so being a hand-built
pattern and running a process were one thing: `OnProductionTick` is abstract and reached every
multiblock through the spent base slot, which made all ten of them processes *by the type system*. A
structure that only has to be built could not be expressed, and a machine needing its base for
anything else - a graph node, a container - could not have a tick at all.

⛔ **The design page's motivating example was false at HEAD.** It argued the seam from
`BlockEntityMultiblockStructure` overriding `OnBlockRemoved` without `OnBlockUnloaded`. It overrides
both, and has since A0 added the second in `97b1e18`. The seam is re-argued from the two things that
are real and were already blocking content: the spent base slot, and the four machine tools each
needing a timed job *and* a window inventory when `BlockEntityProductionMachine` and
`BlockEntityContainer` are two bases.

The tick, its gate, teardown and away-catch-up are now `BEBehaviorProductionMachine`. Three classes
host one, each through the private nested subclass A1's membership introduced:
`BlockEntityProductionMachine` (entire surface kept, 16 descendants untouched),
`BlockEntityMultiblockMachine` (new - the five concrete multiblocks changed one token in their class
declaration, their ten descendants nothing), and `BlockEntityRollingMill`, whose base is spent on
being a graph node. `BlockEntityMultiblockStructure` is form alone, and a multiblock that takes on no
process now compiles and ships with no tick - proved mechanically, by pointing a test double at the
machine base and getting back the exact CS0534 the old edge produced.

**Readiness is three kinds and two levels, and collapsing either loses a shipped behaviour.** The
kinds are pattern completion (`StructureComplete`, ten classes), construction completion
(`IsConstructed`, off the RCC stage count) and peer presence (`Engine != null`); the mill has since
added a fourth, work in hand. Only the first is form, so a form-only provider retires none of the
others - which is why `IProductionReadiness` is published by "whoever knows" rather than by the
structure, and why the process reads `ProductionReadiness.IsReady(Blockentity)` and names no type.
The levels are `IsReadyToProduce` (*may I run this tick*) and `StopsProductionWhenNotReady` (*should
I still be ticking at all*).

**The two aggregates are both `All`, in opposite directions, and the asymmetry is the point.**
Readiness is a conjunction: every publisher must agree, so a machine gated by both a built pattern and
a finished construction is ready only once both say so. Stopping is a veto: one publisher answering
`false` keeps the listener for the whole machine. The two mistakes are not symmetric. A machine left
ticking merely idles; a machine stopped against its will is **frozen with its state held**, because
the gate is only ever read by a listener that still exists. The breached blast furnace is the live
case - it must go on burning its coke and making nothing, not stop mid-campaign - and
`ColdBlastFurnaceScenarioTests` drives the real furnace through it unmodified. A block entity that
publishes nothing is ready, which falls out of `All` over an empty set rather than being asserted;
it is unreachable for every shipped machine and is what a future host that publishes no gate gets.

⛔ **`AutoStartProduction` is deliberately not routed through readiness.** It looks like the next
simplification and it would break eleven machines: auto-start asks *register the listener now?*, and
eleven answer `true` while their readiness is `false` (an unbuilt engine's `IsConstructed`). Reading
readiness there would leave them with no listener to notice the change.

**Teardown ordering is the host's choice, and a hosted capability may not assume one.**
`BlockEntity.OnBlockRemoved` and `.OnBlockUnloaded` both call `UnregisterAllTickListeners()` *before*
fanning out to behaviours, so a host that calls `base` first does the rest of its own teardown with no
listeners left, and one that calls `base` last still has them. Both orderings ship: the multiblock
form tears down after `base`, `BlockEntityFurnaceCore` before it, because its fire must be out while
the tick still exists. So the process's real contribution on those paths is **zeroing its tick
handle**, not unregistering - a handle kept would name a listener that no longer exists and block the
next start - and zeroing is idempotent and side-effect free, which is exactly what makes it safe at
either end. A machine with work to finish does it before calling `base`. Pinned in both directions by
`A_hosted_process_frees_its_handle_from_either_end_of_a_teardown`.

**Persistence did not move, and there is no migration.** `pm_lastHours` is still written and read by
the host block entity, because vanilla fans a behaviour's tree into the block entity's *own flat
tree*: two writers of one key means one silent loser. The process exposes the stamp through
`LastTickHours` / `RestoreLastTickHours` and overrides neither tree method. `structureComplete` and
`pm_lastHours` swapped order within the tree when the edge was cut; a tree is keyed, not ordered, and
nothing reads either while writing the other.

The zero-capability port forwarders are gone. `BlockEntityProductionMachine.ConnectedNetwork<TNet>` /
`NetworkAt<TNet>` did nothing `MachinePorts` does not do as an extension on any `BlockEntity`, and two
shipped machines already used the extension with no production base. ⛔ There were **six** call sites,
not the four a multiblock-only sweep found: `BlockEntityEngine` reaches them twice and is not a
multiblock. `BlockEntityEngineSubmachine`'s non-generic same-named wrapper still resolves to the
extension, and a successful compile is the proof rather than silence - binding to its own non-generic
member would be CS0308.

### The rolling mill, and what an uncapped `dt` was actually doing

The mill loses all three axes at once, so it is the proof consumer. It now adds a
`BEBehaviorProductionMachine` in its constructor, states `ProductionTickMs => 250` on it, and
publishes its gate (`IsReadyToProduce => IsRolling`) instead of testing it at the top of the tick.
⛔ It answers `StopsProductionWhenNotReady => false`: nothing else watches the rolls, so a stand that
gave its clock up when it emptied would never draw the next piece through. All 123 existing mill tests
pass unmodified, which is the parity result - and is also the finding, because **not one of them ever
drove the mill's listener**. Every file calls `AdvancePass` directly with a `dt` of its own choosing.

The hand-rolled listener passed the engine's `dt` through unbounded, and at 250 ms the two halves of
that bug bracket a single crossover. A pass is authored to take about two seconds at full speed, and
travel is `ω·R·dt`, so **any** stall over a second or so draws the whole piece through in one step -
a free finished pass for a stand nobody was standing at. Past about 41 s the other half takes over:
cooling is `T ← ambient + (T−ambient)·e^(−rate·dt)`, and one step that long drops a piece from 1100 °C
below the 900 °C rolling heat, so `travelled` goes to zero and the pass **freezes cold in the rolls**,
where no amount of drive torque recovers it and only the wrench does. Both were reproduced by
mutation, not argued: removing the clamp turns
`A_stalled_server_cannot_push_a_whole_piece_through_the_rolls` and
`A_stalled_server_cannot_chill_the_piece_in_the_rolls_in_one_step` red on exactly those two symptoms.
The clamp is `interval × MaxCatchupTickMultiple`, so the mill's bound is **0.5 s, not 2 s** - which
also means the 250 ms interval is load-bearing twice over, and mutating it to the 1000 ms default
reddens four of the six new tests.

⛔ The exposure is a server stall with the chunk resident - an autosave, a GC pause, a single-player
game left paused - not a rejoin. A block entity's listener is re-stamped on chunk load; it is the
network listener, registered once at startup, that sees every stall for the whole uptime.

### A guard, and one judgement

`AwayCatchupStampTests` is new: a file that hosts a `BEBehaviorProductionMachine` must round-trip
`pm_lastHours`, or carry `no away-catch-up:` and a reason. The stamp is the only thing the catch-up has
to measure an absence against and the behaviour cannot save it itself, so the wiring is per-host
boilerplate that fails silently in both directions - a host that saves nothing has its
`MaxAwayCatchupSteps` honoured and replays nothing, and a host that saves it twice loses a writer
without a word. It is a corpus rule rather than an assertion on one class because the class that will
get it wrong has not been written yet: it is the next machine tool. Sibling rules keep the opt-out
marker from outliving the host it explains or arriving without a reason, the same trap
`TeardownSymmetryTests` already guards for its own.

The other single-test mutation - a multiblock host whose `AutoStartProduction` stops reading the save
tree, which would stop every complete furnace, boiler and converter in every save from ticking on
reload and never restart them, since the monitor tick acts on a *transition* and a machine that loads
complete never makes one - gets no guard. There is no textual or reflective rule that separates a
correct `AutoStartProduction` from an incorrect one; the answer is a per-machine judgement, and the
test that pins it (`A_multiblock_that_loads_already_complete_ticks_without_a_transition`) asserts the
property directly. A second test of the same property would add a copy, not cover.

Cleared alongside: `Interact`'s stop arm on the multiblock form was missing the
`StopsProductionOnStructureLost` guard its monitor-tick twin has - unreachable through the shipped
route, so no player-visible change, but it now asks the same question through the same contract. The
line citations into `BlockEntityProductionMachine.cs` were swept across the eight design pages that
carry them, and those into `BlockEntityMultiblockStructure.cs` across `multiblock.md`; the members
that moved to the behaviour changed file, not just line. `boring-machine.md`'s "the timed job and the
window inventory are two bases" gotcha is retired, and the pages for the heading and nail benches now
model their stroke tick on a hosted process rather than on a hand-registered listener.

Tests: exlib 1471 -> 1637, iwex 1553 -> 1577; lpex 182, hpex 54 and smex 243 unchanged to
the test, which is the parity result. Green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22, and `test-floors.txt` is
ratcheted to the discovered counts - the one ratchet for the whole seam.


## 2026-08-11 - network membership is a behaviour on the cell, not a block base class

The whole of it, over the entries below. The block-network walk used to resolve a node by asking
`world.GetBlock(pos) is BlockNetworkNode`, so being on a network cost a block its single C# base. A
megastructure could not be a node, a footprint cell could not be a node however many ports it hosted,
and a block that had spent its base elsewhere could only reach a network through `INetworkConnector` -
a connection target, never a member. The same filler cell could already join the **vanilla** mechanical
network, because vanilla resolves its nodes by behaviour; one cell, two networks, one reachable and one
not, and the only difference was how the walk found a node.

Membership now belongs to the cell. `INetworkMember` is the source-side contract - `NetworkType`,
`NetworkTypeAt`, `HasConnectorAt`, `IsNetworkEndPoint`, `IsConnectionBroken`, `AcceptsNeighbour` - and
`NetworkMembership.Resolve` answers it in two arms: a `BEBehaviorNetworkMember` on the block entity
whose network type matches, else the block through `INetworkConnector`. `GetConnectedNeighbors` is the
only walk there is, both sides of an edge go through the same resolver, and no traversal names a block
class. `BlockEntityNetworkNode` keeps its whole surface and hosts a private nested membership that
forwards to it, so no concrete block entity was touched. A footprint cell declaring
`exlib.BEBehaviorNetworkMember` is a real graph node, which retires `multiblock.md`'s "a filler can
never be a graph node" rule and, with it, the reason `BlockRollingMillAxle` exists.

**Persistence did not move, and there is no migration.** Vanilla fans behaviour persistence out over
the block entity's *own flat tree* with no subtree, so a behaviour that wrote anything would collide
with the keys the block entity already owns. Exactly one thing may write them and it stays the block
entity - which also keeps `BlockEntityPipe`'s conditional keys and its rewrite of `temp`/`medium`/
`pressure` after `base.ToTreeAttributes` working unchanged. Nothing on disk moved, and `SaveFormatTests`
pins that against a hand-built pre-membership tree: the exact key set, the five keys
`IsNetworkStateMeaningful` gates, and which of the two writers reaches the shared three last. ⛔ A
migration could not have been expressed anyway - `BlockMigrationModSystem` drops any pair whose old and
new block codes are equal.

⛔ `possibleOrientations` stays a single string holding `System.Text.Json` output. It is not a
string-array attribute, `ITreeAttribute` has no array accessor to switch it to, and switching it would
read back empty on every world already on disk, losing players' wrench-rotation choices with nothing to
show for it.

Four rulings that cost an experiment each:

- **The resolver has two arms because a block with no block entity in a loaded chunk is a real state.**
  `BlockConverterIntake` is an `INetworkConnector` that is deliberately not a node and has no block
  entity, 26 fixtures across all five test projects place a node block without one, and a block entity
  can be torn down while its chunk stays resident. ⛔ A chunk *unload* is not that case: it takes the
  block too, both arms answer nothing, and the cell reads as empty space. The graph answers that
  separately, by suspending its fracture check.
- **`GetOpenConnectorFaces` deliberately does not apply `IsNetworkEndPoint` or `IsConnectionBroken` to
  its source, though the walk does.** Making the two agree passes all 15 targets and causes three
  regressions a suite cannot see: a closed molten tap tesselates a second end cap over the one it draws
  itself, a solidified canal grows caps mid-run, and a pressure valve's coupled face is offered to the
  vent classifier as an open end. Whether a face is open is a physical fact, not a graph one.
- **A filler cell's `Block` is one shared always-north singleton**, so a membership there states its own
  connector faces and takes its face from the footprint declaration, pre-rotated into the placed
  orientation. A `connectors` string is written in the unrotated frame and does not turn with the
  structure.
- **A per-cell answer must be a plain public class member.** A block declaring the pair as an *explicit*
  `INetworkMember.Member(...)` is outranked and never called; a `BEBehaviorNetworkMember` *subclass*
  declaring a fresh matching member never enters the interface map, because mapping is fixed at the
  class that lists the interface. Both wrong shapes compile. Every member a subclass may answer
  differently is therefore a `public virtual` on the behaviour itself.

Cleared alongside: `NetworkNodeContract` gained the rule that a declared membership must name its
`networkType` - nothing else can supply one, and a cell without it logs an error and joins no graph -
proved against defs written for it, since no shipped def declares a membership yet. Its existing
placement rule keeps its `BlockNetworkNode` filter deliberately: that failure is that base class's
machinery end to end, and a membership-bearing plain block owes no `type` variant group.
`BlockNetworkModSystem.SidesToFaces` is now the one letters-to-faces walk both a node block's variant
and a membership's declared faces go through. `IsValidNonNetworkConnection` stays, with its doc saying
plainly that nothing overrides it and that the passthrough's "seals against a wall" is really delivered
by the leak classifier's air test. The `ppex`→`lpex` rename left three directories holding nothing but
stale `bin/`+`obj/` - `src/PipesAndPowerExpanded/`, `test/PipesAndPowerExpanded.Tests/` and
`test/Integration.Tests/`, none tracked, none in the solution - and they are gone.


## 2026-08-11 - an unloaded chunk suspends the fracture check instead of answering it

The graph walk could not tell "there is nothing here" from "I cannot see here". `GetBlock` answers the
air block for an unloaded chunk rather than null and `GetBlockEntity` answers null, so an unloaded cell
resolves to no member at all - exactly like an empty one. `RemoveNode`'s fracture check read that as
gone and split the run around it, and it stayed split: the returning cell finds its position already in
a network and never re-joins. A player would see a steam or molten line that quietly stopped carrying
past the point they had walked away from, with the far half shredded into one network per cell.

⛔ The block arm of `NetworkMembership.Resolve` does **not** cover this, and two doc comments said it
did. An unload takes the block with the block entity; the block arm keeps a cell walkable across a
block entity that is absent on its own, which is a different case. Both comments are corrected and
`An_unloaded_cell_answers_for_nothing_even_though_its_block_is_placed` pins the real behaviour.

The fracture check is now **suspended rather than answered** when the walk comes up short and any node
of the network is unreadable: the network is left whole, the positions it could not read are recorded
against it, and `ServerTick` re-decides once at least one of them is back. Three choices worth naming:

- **Positions are recorded, not chunk coordinates.** `GetChunkAtBlockPos(pos)` is dimension-aware by
  contract, while deriving a chunk key by hand means reproducing the engine's convention - which
  vanilla's mechanical-power version of this gets wrong, dividing raw `.Y` rather than `.InternalY`.
  Several missing chunks are just several positions, and each review recomputes the set from scratch.
- **The tick drives it, not `Event.ChunkDirty`.** A chunk event can fire while that chunk's block
  entities are not back yet, and a footprint cell answers through nothing else, so a review in that
  window would read the chunk as loaded and the cell as absent and split a healthy run. The tick cannot
  land inside a load, needs no reason filter and no module-level "everything is loaded" flag - the pair
  that lets vanilla's re-trigger go stale - and a missed event cannot strand a run.
- **A network entirely behind unloaded chunks falls out of the same rule**, not a special case.

The trade-off is deliberate and recorded: while a run is suspended, a break that really did cut it
keeps both halves sharing one pool until the chunk returns. That is strictly better than shredding the
unloaded half permanently.

`TestWorld` gained real chunk modelling - `UnloadChunkAt` / `LoadChunkAt` / `IsChunkLoaded` over
32-block chunks, gating what the accessor sees while leaving the store intact. ⛔ Distinct from the
existing `Unload`, which is the block-entity half and leaves the cell readable. `Tick` now drives the
manager's real `ServerTick`.

Two gaps found and left alone, both recorded in `pipe-network.md` as gotchas 17 and 18: `AddNode`
beside an unloaded chunk never merges with what is over there (pre-existing, and separate - suspension
defers a *split*), and `RebuildFromRoot` drops the nodes it cannot see (self-healing through `AddNode`
on reload).

## 2026-08-11 - a mega-block footprint cell can be a graph node

`BEBehaviorNetworkMember` carries `[BlockEntityBehaviorRegister]` and implements
`IFillerHostedBehavior`, which is the whole of what stood between the flipped walk and the payoff: a
`fillerOffsets` cell declaring `exlib.BEBehaviorNetworkMember` now joins the graph and bridges two
nodes on opposite sides of itself. Without the attribute the class registry could not build it, so the
`networkType` and `connectors` support written for it was unreachable. A cell that declares no
membership is still not a node - the limitation is gone, not inverted.

`ConfigureFromFiller` takes the pre-rotated face as the cell's connector set, and `passThrough` adds
its opposite so a run passes straight through the cell, the axis rule the MP port already follows. It
is opt-in: an automatic opposite would open the far side of every single-face port, which for the
boiler would mean a pipe laid against the back of it tapping the steam. A `connectors` string is not
rotated by the filler pipeline, so a rotating footprint must state its `face` instead - documented
where a reader will hit it.

**Membership beats port where both name the same network.** A footprint cell can now answer through
both arms of the resolver - a membership on its block entity and the `PortFace`/`PortNetworkType` a
principal writes onto that same block entity. The membership wins: it is the cell's own participation
and the thing that registered the node, so a port cannot move a node's faces. Per network type the two
compose - a membership answers for the network it names, the port for any other - and a membership
that states no face falls through to the port's, which upgrades an existing port cell to a node
without restating its geometry. `INetworkConnector` keeps meaning a port, unchanged.

Two defects found on the way, both fixed here:

- Behaviour detach was a bare `Behaviors.Remove`, so a detached membership never deregistered and left
  a graph node at a position nothing owned. It is now told `OnBlockRemoved` first. Re-applying the
  same declaration set is a no-op rather than a teardown, so an unchanged declaration cannot cost a
  cell its node and a re-merge of the run around it.
- `GetConnectedNetworkAcross` still resolved the far cell as a block, so a machine reading across a
  face into a footprint cell that is a node would find no network. It asks `NetworkMembership.CouplesAt`
  now - memberships then the block, strictly more than it saw before.

⛔ A footprint cell's participation lives entirely on its block entity, where an ordinary node block
goes on answering from the block while its chunk is unloaded. A run bridged *through* a footprint cell
can therefore fracture while that chunk is away and stay fractured until the next topology change.
Nothing ships with a filler membership yet, so no world is affected today; suspending discovery for an
unloaded chunk is the fix.

⛔ `BlockStructureFiller.HasMechPowerConnectorAt` reads every hosted declaration's face rather than an
MP one's, so a cell hosting both a membership and an MP port would offer an axle the membership's face
too. No shipped cell hosts both. Recorded in `multiblock.md`, not fixed.

`BlockRollingMillAxle` is now redundant - a `passThrough` membership on the mill's two drive cells does
the same job - but the block stays: it is placed in existing worlds and removing it needs a migration.
`multiblock.md`'s retired section is rewritten and its can/cannot table deleted; the five pages that
cited the old invariant (`framework-composition`, `boring-machine`, `twin-tub-blower`, `boiler-cornish`,
`rolling-mill`) are corrected where they asserted it, and the citations in the sections touched are
re-checked.

## 2026-08-11 - the block-network walk resolves a node by membership, not by block type

`BlockNetworkModSystem` no longer names `BlockNetworkNode` on any node-resolution path. Both sides of
the walk now go through `NetworkMembership.Resolve`: the source in `GetConnectedNeighbors` and in
`RebuildFromRoot`, and the neighbour in `IsValidNetworkNeighbour`, which used to be gated on the
block-level `INetworkConnector`. Both had to move together - `INetworkConnector` is a block interface,
so flipping only the source would leave a membership-bearing plain block walkable *from* and never
*to*, which is a network that exists in one direction only.

`IsValidNetworkNeighbour` gained the source `BlockPos` it never had, so the source's network type is
read per cell (`NetworkTypeAt(world, sourcePos)`) rather than block-wide. Without it a per-cell source
- which is what a structure filler is - cannot be expressed at all. `GetConnectedNeighbors`'s
`networkType` argument was dead, the walk having read `sourceNode.NetworkType` instead; it is now what
selects the membership. Every production caller already passed the cell's own type.

`GetOpenConnectorFaces` takes an `INetworkMember` rather than a `BlockNetworkNode`. All three callers
compile unchanged, a node block being a member.

⛔ The two consumers of `IsValidNetworkNeighbour` gate their **source** differently, and that stays.
The traversal refuses to walk out of an endpoint or a severed cell; the open-end scan does not ask.
Making them agree was tried and reverted: whether a face is open is a physical fact, not a graph one.
A closed tap severs itself and draws its own end cap over its single connector, so the base's scan
would cap the same face a second time; a solidified canal mid-run would grow caps where it physically
meets its neighbours; a pressure valve, being an endpoint, would report its coupled face as an open
end. Nothing in the suite covered any of the three - the strict version passed all 15 targets - so
`ConnectorTests` now pins both gates, and `pipe-network.md` § 1 records why they are asymmetric.

## 2026-08-11 - a network membership can state its own connector faces

A membership inherited its faces from its block and could state none of its own, so one on a plain
`Block` - which is no `INetworkConnector` - answered false for every face and could couple to nothing.
That is the cell the whole membership axis exists for, and it was unbuildable.

`BEBehaviorNetworkMember.Connectors` now holds the faces, empty by default. Empty means the block
answers, which is what every shipped node relies on; a non-empty set outranks it, because the two
cases that need one have a block that cannot answer for the cell at all - a plain block, or the single
shared `exlib:structurefiller` singleton a footprint cell sits on. Faces arrive as an orientation
string through `DeclareConnectors` or a JSON `connectors` key, mapped by
`BlockNetworkModSystem.SideToFace`, or as `BlockFacing`s straight onto the property, which is the form
a filler will hand over: its declared face is already rotated into the placed orientation. That
rotation is why a set configured in code beats a JSON declaration and the disagreement is logged - a
declaration is written unrotated, so letting it win would move the port. Same precedence and same
noise as a losing `networkType` declaration.

`TestWorld.PlaceNode` and `TestWorld.PlaceMemberBlock` place a cell and register it through the real
`Initialize`, never a second `AddNode`. `NetworkGraphTests.BuildLine` is rewritten over `PlaceNode`,
which changes the registration order - each cell now registers as it is placed, so the first is
isolated and later ones merge - and no assertion in that file moved. The other 26 bare placements are
deliberately untouched: the resolver's block arm answers for them precisely because a chunk unload
leaves a block placed with no block entity.

⛔ The double-registration hazard is narrower than it reads. `AddNode` *before* the membership
initialises is harmless - the membership skips its own add when the position already has a network -
while `AddNode` *after* builds a whole second network over the cell and strands the first.
`BlockNetwork.Nodes` is a `HashSet`, so a node count never notices; only the network count does.

## 2026-08-11 - a canal piece draws every arm of its footprint, not one picked by fill level

Reported: a tee pouring metal into its side branch never renders the side surface. `MoltenRenderer`
was built with `combine: false` and overrode `SelectMeshIndex` as `(int)(FillRatio * MeshRefs.Length)`
- one mesh per footprint box, and only the one the fill ratio lands on gets drawn. That reading of
`fillQuadsByLevel` (one cross-section per fill *level*, for a cavity that changes shape with height)
has **no shipped consumer**: every molten blocktype declares its quads as a *spatial* footprint, and
the four that declare more than one are exactly the pieces with more than one arm.

So a tee drew its through-channel under half full and only its side stub over it; a bend drew half
its elbow; the start's basin vanished above half full and left the spout; the cross drew one arm of
three, swapping at each third. The quads are authored to abut without overlapping, which is what a
single combined surface wants. `MoltenRenderer` now passes `combine: true` and the override is gone -
a cell holds one pool, so its arms are one surface at one height. `BoilerWaterRenderer` already
combined; the base keeps the per-box capability for whoever needs it.

`Invariants/MoltenSurfaceTests` parses the shipped molten blocktypes through the same
`FillQuads.BoxesFrom` the block entity uses, and asserts each multi-arm piece uploads one mesh. It
fails on all eight (four pieces × brick and cobblestone) with the old flag, and asserts as its
premise that the tee, cross, bend and start still declare multi-box footprints.

⛔ The attribute is still named `fillQuadsByLevel` in twelve blocktypes and in the generated
accessor, which is now a misnomer for what it holds. Renaming it touches every molten blocktype and
the attribute generator, so it was left alone deliberately.

## 2026-08-11 - molten canals convey outward instead of levelling; the 0.9.7 migrations never fired

Players on the published 0.9.6 report molten metal stopping four blocks from the source ("no matter
how much molten iron is available or what temperature it is at", "we can't get it far enough to reach
the bessemer converter", and a third confirming it on a clean install). The screenshot reads
**12/50** on the last wet cell, which is the whole diagnosis: 50 is the pre-rebalance
`CanalDefaultUnitCapacity`, and 12 is what `FlowEdge` settles on at the third cell when
`MoltenMinFlowAmount` is 10. Two separate defects, both shipped in the same release.

**The min-flow floor moved onto the halved step.** `FlowEdge` levels toward the midpoint, so a floor
on the *step* is a floor of twice its value on the *gap* - the opposite of what the comment claimed
it was fixing. With a saved config carrying 10, an edge needed 20 units of head before it moved
anything at all, so a fed run settles at 50, 31, 12, dry: metal reaches the third canal and stops.
The floor is back on the gap.

**Levelling itself caps a run by its length.** Halving the gap means the rate an edge carries is
proportional to its head, so sustaining any delivery costs head at *every* block and a long run pays
it out of one cell's capacity. Even on the rebalanced defaults a 20-block run delivered 65 units
where a 4-block one delivered 160 over the same window, and a single unit could never leave the start
at all (a player reported exactly that). Metal running **away from the start** - into a drain fitting,
or into a cell farther out by the BFS distance the tick already computes - is now conveyed: the giver
hands over its whole surplus, capped only by `MoltenFlowRate` and the receiver's room. Metal levelling
**back** (backfill toward the start, or between two cells the same distance out) still moves to the
midpoint, which is what makes an idle run settle instead of swapping its charge every tick. A tee now
fills the first branch it is asked about before the second, rather than splitting the difference;
both are still served once the first backs up, which is asserted.

**⛔ The release was stamped below its own config migrations, so none of them ran.** `96f3809` set
smex back to 0.9.6 and ppex to 0.6.5 on the way out the door, while the rebalance they shipped is
pushed to existing configs by migrations keyed `ToVersion = "0.9.7"` (and ppex 0.6.6, 0.6.7).
`ApplyMigrations` only crosses a migration whose target is **at or below the running version**, so
every saved `smex_values.json` kept the old numbers while the new code ran against them - the exact
state the 0.9.7 migration comment says must never happen ("a furnace that cannot reach its own melting
point on cold blast"). A fresh install got the new defaults and looked fine, which is why it read as
"the latest patch broke canals". Versions bumped to **smex 0.9.7 / ppex 0.6.7**; smex's ppex floor
goes to 0.6.7 with it, so the two must be published together.

`Invariants/ConfigMigrationTests` is the guard: it scans the referenced mods for a static
`Migrations` array and fails any migration targeting a version above that mod's modinfo. It caught
the ppex pair on its first run. `Networks/MoltenRunLengthTests` covers reach, length-independent
delivery, the stranded last unit, the tee, and a run driven under a saved pre-rebalance config. The
casting fixture's head cell is now a real `BlockEntityMoltenCanalStart` - the only cell anything can
pour into, and what the flow direction is measured from.

## 2026-08-11 - a declared network type loses to the block entity that already has one

**⛔ A JSON `networkType` on a membership whose block entity already names a network is now refused
with an error, not applied.** Applying it was silent in two incompatible ways: sixteen of the
seventeen node families implement `NetworkType` as a constant getter with an empty setter, so the
write vanished and the cell registered under the constant regardless; `BlockEntityFluidIntake`, the
one with a real setter, took the declaration *and* persisted it through
`BlockEntityNetworkNode.ToTreeAttributes`. Same JSON, opposite outcomes, neither visible. A block
entity's type is a compiled contract - a pipe that declared itself molten would be a different
machine - so the block entity wins and the disagreement is logged. The key still applies to a
membership that names nothing of its own, which is the case it exists for. Latent until a membership
can be declared in JSON at all, which is the next task.

**⛔ `redrawOnClient` does not gate the attribute resync.** `BlockEntity.MarkDirty` calls
`MarkBlockEntityDirty` unconditionally and only then, `if (redrawOnClient)`, adds the mesh redraw, so
**every** `MarkDirty(...)` re-runs `FromTreeAttributes` on the client - the bare no-argument call
included. The drift window for anything caching a value out of the tree is therefore much wider than
"on a redraw", and `BlockEntityFluidIntake` calls bare `MarkDirty()` once a second. Corrected in
`framework-composition.md`, which had claimed the narrow version.

The membership's teardown reads `Blockentity.Api` outright. The `?? Api` fallback was dead:
`BlockEntityBehavior.Api` is written only by `BlockEntityBehavior.Initialize`, and both of its callers
guarantee the block entity's is already set - so the comment promising it covered a real case was the
only part doing any work.

**Two guards stopped assuming their own premises.** The attach-only teardown test now asserts that the
membership's `Api` really is null on that path, so a harness that starts initialising behaviours turns
it red instead of silently making it a tautology; and the interface-map guard is asked through an
`INetworkMember` reference against `BlockEntityPipe` rather than through a concrete local against a
double, since a class member answers either way and only the map answers the question. Drift itself is
guarded in lpex against `BlockEntityFluidIntake` through a real tree round trip - the only production
node it can happen to. The logger assertions name the diagnostic and the cell rather than matching any
error at all.

15/15 targets pass (exlib 1559 / iwex 1571 / lpex 182 / hpex 54 / smex 243); floors +2 exlib, +1 lpex.


## 2026-08-11 - four corrections to the membership behaviour

**⛔⛔ A behaviour's `Api` and its block entity's are not the same availability.** Only
`BlockEntityBehavior.Initialize` assigns the behaviour's, while a block entity can be handed one
without ever being initialised - which is precisely the harness pattern thirteen fixtures across
iwex, lpex and smex use (`TestWorld.Attach`, prime `NetworkSystem` by reflection, `AddNode` by hand).
Gating the membership's teardown on the behaviour's `Api` therefore stopped those fixtures
deregistering anything, and **nothing went red**: they assert on what the machine did, not on the
graph. The gate now reads `Blockentity.Api ?? Api` - the block entity's is assigned on the first line
of `BlockEntity.Initialize`, before the behaviour fan-out, so it is set whenever the behaviour's is
and on the attach-only path besides. A test at exactly that shape now covers the pattern.

**⛔ A hosted membership must not hold a copy of its network type.** `FromTreeAttributes` runs again
on every `MarkDirty(true)` client sync, so a value taken at `Initialize` drifts from a node whose type
is a real setter - `BlockEntityFluidIntake`. Harmless while nothing reads it; wrong the moment the
graph walk keys on `NetworkTypeAt`. `NetworkType` is now a `public virtual` on
`BEBehaviorNetworkMember` and the hosted membership overrides both accessors onto its owner, so there
is no copy to drift. `OnBeforeRegister` existed only to sync it and is gone.
`framework-composition.md`'s claim that `NetworkType` "is set once per instance rather than
overridden" was wrong and is corrected.

A membership that names no network now logs an **error** rather than a warning, and still joins
nothing rather than throwing: registering a blank type throws out of the network factory lookup, and
this runs inside a chunk load, so one bad declaration would take a world down - but a cell silently
outside its run is what a player reports as "my pipes stopped working", which is not a caution. No
shipped save can reach it (`ToTreeAttributes` writes the key unconditionally); it becomes reachable
when a JSON declaration can name a membership, which is why the level is the whole fix.

**⛔ The isolated cell is the only one that catches a double registration.** The reload guard now
covers the path the design rests on - an unload keeps the node, so the returning block entity must
find it and skip `AddNode` - and it uses a *single* node deliberately. With a neighbour present a
second `AddNode` merely re-joins the same network and every assertion still passes; alone it takes
the "isolated new node" branch, builds a second network over the same cell and strands the first.

15/15 targets pass (exlib 1557 / iwex 1571 / lpex 181 / hpex 54 / smex 243); exlib floor +4. Three of
the four new tests were watched red first; the reload guard was mutation-checked.


## 2026-08-11 - graph registration moves into the membership behaviour

`BEBehaviorNetworkMember` now registers its own cell: it resolves `BlockNetworkModSystem`, adds the
node on `Initialize`, drops it on `OnBlockRemoved`, and runs the restore-on-load handshake.
`BlockEntityNetworkNode` keeps every public member it had and hosts a private nested subclass of the
behaviour that forwards to it, so no concrete block entity changed and nothing changed visibility - a
nested type reaches its enclosing type's private and protected members. `NetworkSystem` now reads and
writes through that membership rather than holding a second copy, which is what keeps the thirteen
fixtures that prime it by reflection working unchanged.

**Persistence deliberately did not move.** Vanilla fans behaviour persistence out over the block
entity's own flat tree with no subtree, so a block entity and its behaviour writing network state
would write the same keys and the second writer would win. It stays on the block entity, which also
leaves `BlockEntityPipe`'s conditional keys and its rewrite of `temp`/`medium`/`pressure` *after*
`base.ToTreeAttributes` untouched. Nothing on disk changes.

**⛔ Three orderings the engine dictates, each wrong-answer-silently.** The membership is added in the
**constructor**, because `FromTreeAttributes` and `Initialize` both fan out over `Behaviors` and a
later addition misses whichever has already run. It reads its network type at **registration**, never
earlier: the tree fan-out reaches behaviours before the block entity assigns its own `NetworkType`,
and a derived field initializer runs before the base constructor body, so both earlier points are too
early - `BlockEntityFluidIntake`, whose `NetworkType` is a plain auto-property the save tree fills in,
is the case that proves it. And it reads the state to restore **before** `AddNode`, because `AddNode`
broadcasts, the broadcast reaches `OnNetworkUpdate`, and that clears the very state being restored.
Both are virtual hooks (`OnBeforeRegister`, `SavedNetworkState`) the host overrides; a standalone
membership needs neither. The reasoning is in `framework-composition.md` § "A hosted membership
registers, and the engine fixes when".

`properties` is null on the programmatic path - `BlockEntityBehavior.Initialize` sets only `Api`, and
only `CreateBehaviors` assigns `properties` - so every read of it is guarded, while a JSON declaration
can still name its network type through `networkType`. A membership that ends up naming no network at
all logs and joins nothing rather than throwing out of a chunk load; the old base class could not
reach that state, an instantiable behaviour can.

**⛔ The `removal-only teardown:` opt-out moved with the code that owns it**, and
`TeardownSymmetryTests` gained a rule that it cannot be left behind. The guard reads any file carrying
the marker as exempt, so a marker still sitting on a class that no longer tears anything down would
have quietly exempted whatever landed in that file next - which is exactly the state this change
would have created. The guard already covered behaviours, since a behaviour's `OnBlockRemoved()` has
the same signature as a block entity's; that is now said out loud in its doc, because it is
load-bearing rather than incidental.

15/15 test targets pass (exlib 1553 / iwex 1571 / lpex 181 / hpex 54 / smex 243); exlib floor +6. The
three tests a standalone membership makes possible were watched red first; the two that guard
behaviour the base class already had - registering under the concrete node's own network type, and
the restore surviving a reload beside a live run - were mutation-checked instead, as was the teardown
marker.


## 2026-08-11 - network membership gets an abstraction, and it has two arms

`INetworkMember` is the whole contract the block-network graph walk needs from a cell: its network
type, its connectors, whether it is an endpoint, whether it currently severs the run, and whether it
accepts a given neighbour. `INetworkConnector` now extends it, so a block answers directly with no
adapter, and `AcceptsNeighbour`/`IsNetworkEndPoint` - which lived only on `BlockNetworkNode` - are
part of the shared contract. `NetworkMembership` resolves a cell to a member, and
`BEBehaviorNetworkMember` is the block-entity-side answer. The walk itself is untouched.

**The resolver asks the block entity first and the block second, and the second arm is not
redundancy.** "Network block present, block entity absent" is a live state, not a test shortcut:
`BlockEntityNetworkNode.OnBlockUnloaded` deliberately does not `RemoveNode`, so a chunk unload
leaves the block placed with its graph node intact and no block entity behind it. A behaviour-only
resolver would find no membership there, yield no neighbours, and split the network every time a
player walked away. The block arm also keeps `BlockConverterIntake` working - an `INetworkConnector`
that is deliberately not a node and has no block entity at all.

Selection is by network type, not by CLR type: `GetBehavior<T>()` returns the first match of a type,
which cannot tell a pipe membership from a molten one on the same block entity. `MemberOf` keys on
`NetworkType` so one block entity can sit on several networks at once. Both arms of `Resolve` compare
`NetworkTypeAt(world, pos)` through one shared predicate, never the declared type: a membership whose
type varies by cell, which is what a filler cell is, would otherwise be unfindable at a position
where the equivalent block is found.

**⛔ Two ways to write a per-cell answer that compiles and is never called.** Both are now in
`framework-composition.md` and cited from `INetworkMember`, because neither is visible at the call
site and each costs a day to rediscover. An *explicit* `bool INetworkMember.HasConnectorAt(...)` on
an implementing class cannot be overridden by anything, so a subclass's per-cell answer is stranded;
a class member must be plain `public`, which then outranks the interface default. And interface
mapping is fixed at the class that *lists* the interface, so a fresh member on a subclass of
`BEBehaviorNetworkMember` never enters the map and the base keeps answering. That is why the
behaviour declares every interface member as a `public virtual`, `NetworkTypeAt` included, rather
than inheriting any as a default. `BlockNetworkNode`'s doc on the position-aware `HasConnectorAt`
said it "implements the `INetworkConnector` default interface method", which named the wrong
interface and inverted the precedence; it now says the class member is what makes an override
reachable at all.

## 2026-08-10 - the vanilla source is mapped; the 0.9 fixes start coming across

The public game source was vendored at `.compat/vintagestory/` and is now mapped into
`docs/internal/vanilla/`: where every type lives, the practices vanilla follows, and the traps its source
hides - each cited to a `repo/path.cs:line` that can be checked in one step. Two things inside
`vsapi` that the directory names hide and nothing else documents: `docs/api/` is the full generated
API reference, and `docs/json-docs/` is the **JSON asset schema** - what every blocktype, itemtype,
shape and recipe field means. Neither exists anywhere else.

**⛔ A test that passed by doing nothing.** `MoldSpecTests.A_missing_output_is_rejected` cut the
`output` line out of a valid spec by searching for a string ending `},\n`. The file is CRLF, so the
cut never landed, the "malformed" spec was the valid one, and the assertion had been passing on the
wrong thing on every Windows checkout. It failed here only because the rejection is real. The cut no
longer matches a line terminator, and it asserts that it landed.

**Two invariant suites that 0.9-support has and this branch never got**, since `Integration.Tests`
was dissolved in the split. Rebuilt against the consolidated `assets/` tree and the code-first
definitions, they found eight live defects on the first run. Seven shapes - all of `mpenergy`, the
rolling mill, and the lpex MP generator - ended a `Repeat` clip on a duplicate of frame 0, so the
one-frame wrap unwound the whole revolution backwards; the machine snapped to its unbuilt pose once
per cycle. Vanilla ends a turn one frame short (`jonas/gencore.json`: `-348` over 30 frames with
`rotShortestDistance`), which is `360 * (frames-1) / frames`. The generator's fix is a straight
backport - 0.9-support already ships `174.0`, this branch still had `180.0`.

That shape rule is one half of a pair, and **the other half was still wrong here**:
`MPAnim.FrameFromAngle` spanned `totalFrames - 1`, which maps a revolution onto everything except
the wrap segment. `CycleAnimProgress` and `PhaseLockToEngine` had the same off-by-one in the
engine-to-sub-machine direction. Both fixed, `LockFrameToAngle` brought over, and the formula in
`engine-watt.md` corrected - it documented the old one.

**The transmission had no shape at all, in all twelve variants.** `SideVariant()` emits `n/e/s/w`
but `BlockTransmission` kept a local `["north","east","south","west"]` to build its `shapeByType`
wildcards, so every selector missed. It now reads `ExOrientations.Face.Tokens`, which is the list
`SideVariant` expands, so the wildcard cannot drift from the codes it selects again.

**A guard per silent failure.** `ShippedAssetJsonTests` parses every shipped asset and rejects
control characters; `EmittedBlocktypeShapeTests` checks every golden variant resolves a shape;
`LangKeyResolutionTests` checks every literal `Lang.Get` key exists, since `TranslationService`
returns the key verbatim on a miss; `ModinfoTests` checks the packager can actually rewrite the
`game` dependency. Each asserts its own premise as a separate fact - a corpus that silently matches
nothing turns a `[Theory]` into a pass that reads like a clean run.

`ModinfoTests` found two live ones. iwex declared `"game": "1.21"` while the packager rewrites
per-target by a literal replace of `"game": "1.22.0"`, so **every iwex zip shipped claiming 1.21**,
including the 1.20 one. And four mods floored their `exlib` dependency at `0.1.0` against an exlib
that is `0.7.0`; a dependency is a minimum, so the game would happily load a sibling far too old to
carry the APIs. `PackageTask` now throws on a modinfo it cannot rewrite rather than reporting
success on a replace that matched nothing.

**From the 0.9-support audit** - 132 findings, 67 upheld after adversarial verification, 16 of the
83 "missing" claims refuted on inspection. Applied so far: the filler port read `Network.Speed`
without `GearedRatio` (a network holds one speed, in the frame of whichever node seeded it, so a
geared machine reported the drive's speed or its own depending on chunk load order);
`ExContentGate` nulled `CreativeInventoryTabs`, which vanilla dereferences without a null check in
`BehaviorAttachable`; the orientation relaxation passed single letters to `BlockFacing.FromCode`,
which only knows the full words, so the whole fallback was dead and a pipe against a wall broke
itself instead of relaxing; a multiblock's angle was never primed at load, so the first production
ticks resolved every structure-local offset unrotated; the config register was written by both
sides and raced with itself in singleplayer.

**Two from the player comments on the mod DB.** A pipe carrying its whole load still read "Empty"
here - the throughput line was gated on the medium label, which clears with the last litre, so the
pipes working hardest looked dead (the reported case was a tuyere in balance with its blower). And
the piston stroke sounds fired on every keyframe on nearly every tick whenever a cycle ran
backwards, because a reversed cycle and a loop wrap are indistinguishable by the sign of the delta
alone; that is the "ton of bells clicking" on the Watt engine. Both fixed.

⛔ The `pipe-medium-unknown` string is `газа`, genitive, not the nominative `Газ` that 0.9-support
shipped - the Russian line reads "{0} {1} при {2}" and its four siblings are all genitive. Worth
fixing on 0.9-support too.

**The pressure valve's vent readout never reached the client.** `_lastVentVolume` was set only by
the server-side tick and `ToTreeAttributes` never carried it, so `GetBlockInfo` - which also runs
client-side - read a permanent zero there and the overflow line could never show. Now written and
read alongside `gatePressure`. The `MarkDirty(true)` on a changed vent volume forced a chunk
re-tesselation for a value the mesh never depended on; changed to `MarkDirty()`, which still syncs
the block entity without the redraw.

**A destroyed megastructure left its filler cells behind.** `BlockFilledMegastructure` cleared its
footprint only from `OnBlockBroken`, which nothing but a player break calls; an explosion
(`Block.OnBlockExploded` sets the block to air through the bulk accessor) or a worldedit delete
skipped it entirely, leaving invisible, solid, unbreakable orphan cells. The cleanup now runs from
`OnBlockRemoved`, which the engine calls on every removal path, matching vanilla's
`BlockLargeGear3m`. Checked for the re-entrancy the move risks: `BlockStructureFiller.OnBlockBroken`
forwards a filler break to the principal's `OnBlockBroken`, but `RemoveFillers` clears cells through
`SetBlock`, which only reaches `OnBlockRemoved` - a hook `BlockStructureFiller` does not override -
so the forward never loops back into the cleanup it triggered.

**Teardown ran on removal but not on chunk unload.** Eight block entities overrode `OnBlockRemoved`
with no `OnBlockUnloaded`, so everything the removal path released was skipped whenever a chunk left
memory. `TeardownSymmetryTests` now enforces the pair across `src`, matching the argument-less block
entity signatures so the `Block` overloads of the same names are out of scope. A file whose asymmetry
is deliberate opts out with `removal-only teardown:` and the reason, which a second guard checks is
actually written. The check is per file, not per class tree: a subclass's teardown is its own, and a
corrected base class does not run it.

The one real leak was `BlockEntityMultiblockStructure`. Its build outline lives in a per-player
highlight slot owned by the world rather than by the block entity, so an unload that skipped the
clear painted a projection over ground that no longer resolves; it now clears on both paths, as
vanilla's `BEBeeHiveKiln` does (`vssurvivalmod/BlockEntity/BEBeeHiveKiln.cs:536`, `:559`).

Four more turned out to need deletion rather than an addition. The tall hopper, the bell hopper, the
fluid intake and the pressure valve each overrode `OnBlockRemoved` to unregister one tick listener by
handle - which vanilla's `OnBlockRemoved` already does wholesale through `UnregisterAllTickListeners`
(`vsapi/Common/Collectible/Block/BlockEntity.cs:212`), as its `OnBlockUnloaded` does at `:261`. The
override was dead on the path it was written for, so writing its unload twin would have doubled the
dead code rather than fixed anything. Both hooks are gone from all four, along with the three
listener handles no longer read; the bell keeps its `_tickId`, since `IsDropping` genuinely starts
and stops that tick. Removing both hooks also drops those files out of the guard's corpus, so none
of them needs a marker.

⛔ Three are removal-only by design and say so in the file. `BlockEntityNetworkNode.OnBlockRemoved`
calls `RemoveNode`, and `BlockEntitySmokeStack` does the same by hand for the gas graph it registers
itself into; deregistering on unload would fracture a live network every time a player walked away
from it. `BlockEntityFurnaceCore` extinguishes a running furnace on removal, which freezes the pool
and burns the charge out to residue - on unload the furnace is still lit and the production machine's
away-catch-up replays the time it missed, so extinguishing there would put out every furnace whose
player left the chunk.

**The design table's drafting window outlived its block.** `BlockEntityDesignTable` created
`GuiDialogDesignTable` in `ToggleDialog` and cleared the reference only from the dialog's own
`OnClosed` handler; nothing closed it on removal or unload. Break the table with the window open, or
walk away until the chunk unloads, and the GUI stayed on screen bound to a dead block entity, and its
eventual close sent a block-entity packet to a position with nothing there to receive it. An `internal
virtual CloseDialog` now closes the dialog if open, disposes it and nulls the field, called from both
`OnBlockRemoved` and `OnBlockUnloaded` after their `base` calls - the same shape as vanilla's
`BEOpenableContainer.Dispose` (`vssurvivalmod/BlockEntity/BEOpenableContainer.cs:264-269`).
`GuiDialogDesignTable` needs a real `ICoreClientAPI`, which the headless test world does not provide,
so the dialog itself is never opened in test; coverage instead spies on `CloseDialog` through a
test-only subclass (visible to the internal member via `InternalsVisibleTo`) that counts its calls,
which catches an override reduced to nothing but its `base` call - the dead-teardown shape the
`TeardownSymmetryTests` regex cannot see once both overrides are still present - and a second case
asserts the field stays null and nothing throws when no dialog was ever open, the common path.

**`IronworkingExpanded.Tests` was discovering zero tests on some runs, silently.** vstest's own
assembly resolver probes a test assembly's direct references on disk before the harness's
`VsAssemblyResolver` gets a turn to answer from the `.game` install; `VSSurvivalMod` was not copied
