into the output folder, so the probe's first attempt threw `ReflectionTypeLoadException` during
discovery's type scan and the whole assembly was skipped ("could not find dependent assembly
'VSSurvivalMod'") - the same trap already fixed for `VintagestoryAPI` in this project, now extended to
`VSSurvivalMod` by copying it into the output folder too, which makes discovery deterministic instead
of a race. Confirmed byte-identical to its source in each of the three `.game/<slug>` installs, so the
copy shadows the resolver with exactly the file it would have returned anyway. Anywhere a suite reports
zero tests without an error, this is the first thing to check. The same trap is still latent for
`VSEssentials` in this suite and for the four other test suites, whose output folders carry no game
dlls at all; all discover tests correctly today, so nothing is broken yet, and `scripts/test-floors.txt`
is what would catch it if the race ever turned against one of them.

**Eight block entities stored a stack without mapping its collectible.** `ItemStack.ToBytes`
serialises the collectible's runtime id, not its code, so a stack pasted into another world via a
schematic resolved whatever item happened to hold that id there - the wrong metal, the wrong mold,
or nothing at all. None of the eight turned out to be inventory-backed; every one holds its stack in
a loose field, so all follow the shape `BlockEntityMoltenBarrel` and `BlockEntityCastMold` already
had: `OnStoreCollectibleMappings` forwards each stack's `Collectible.OnStoreCollectibleMappings`
through a `DummySlot`, `OnLoadCollectibleMappings` calls `ItemStack.FixMapping`. The mold pedestal
and the canal tap map two and three parked stacks respectively (mold, barrel, cast metal); the
rolling mill maps the piece under the rolls and the fitted roll set; the heating hearth maps all
three row slots; the two tall/reinforced/bell hoppers and the converter's molten bath (via
`MoltenCharge.Stack`) each map one. A repo-wide guard
(`test/ExpandedLib.Tests/Invariants/CollectibleMappingGuardTests.cs`) now fails any
`class BlockEntity` that calls `SetItemstack(` without pairing it with
`OnStoreCollectibleMappings`, matching all eight before the fix and none after. One behavioural test
(`test/IronworkingExpanded.Tests/Blocks/CollectibleMappingTests.cs`) rounds the simplest of the eight
- the rolling mill, reachable through the public `BeginPass` with no multiblock or network setup -
through `OnStoreCollectibleMappings` into a dictionary and checks the collectible's code lands
against its id.

**Five more blocks hand-rolled the same filler cleanup Task 2 had already moved off the break
path**, unable to extend `BlockFilledMegastructure` because each had already spent its one base
class on `BlockNetworkNode`, `BlockPipe` or plain `Block`: `BlockFlywheel`, `BlockRollingMill`,
`BlockTwinTubMPBlower`, `BlockManualFluidPump` and `BlockConverterBessemer`. Each called
`StructureFillers.RemoveFillers` only from its own `OnBlockBroken`, so an explosion or a worldedit
delete still left the reserved footprint behind. Each now clears it from an added
`OnBlockRemoved(world, pos)` instead. Three of the five (`BlockFlywheel`, `BlockTwinTubMPBlower`,
`BlockManualFluidPump`) had nothing break-specific left in `OnBlockBroken` once the call moved out,
so the override is gone rather than kept as a bare `base` call. The other two keep what belongs on
the break path: `BlockRollingMill` still frees its two axle nodes there, the
`world.Side == EnumAppSide.Server` guard preserved around both that call and the moved
`RemoveFillers` rather than lifted; `BlockConverterBessemer` still collects and spawns its
solidified charge and degrades a construction-drop throw to a plain removal. `BlockBoiler` was
already correct - it derives from the fixed `BlockFilledMegastructure`, so `BlockEntityBoiler`'s
explicit `RemoveStructure` call ahead of its own `SetBlock(0, pos)` in `Explode` is a harmless
pre-emptive duplicate of the inherited `OnBlockRemoved`, since `RemoveFillers` no-ops on a cell that
does not hold a filler for that principal. A repo-wide guard
(`test/ExpandedLib.Tests/Invariants/FillerCleanupHookTests.cs`) fails any file calling
`RemoveFillers` from an `OnBlockBroken` override with no `OnBlockRemoved` override alongside it; it
first also named `BlockStructureFiller.cs`, which only mentions `RemoveFillers` in a comment
describing the principal's own cleanup and never calls it, so the match narrowed to the call syntax
(`RemoveFillers(`) rather than the bare method name.

**The whole-branch review's two Important findings, closed.** `BlockRollingMill.RemoveAxleNodes` was
still break-only after the guard above shipped - the axle cells are also live `mpenergy` graph nodes,
so exploding or worldedit-deleting a mill left two invisible, solid, unbreakable cells stranded inside
the network. `FillerCleanupHookTests` had certified the file clean, because its check was file-wide:
it saw an `OnBlockRemoved` override existed and stopped looking, never checking which cleanup call was
actually inside it. The guard now reads each method's own body, brace-matched from its signature, and
checks call by call that anything reachable from `OnBlockBroken` is also reachable from the `Block`
overload of `OnBlockRemoved` - confirmed red against the pre-fix file, naming
`BlockRollingMill.cs (RemoveAxleNodes)`, before the fix landed. `RemoveAxleNodes` now runs beside
`RemoveFillers` under the same server guard in `OnBlockRemoved`; the now-empty `OnBlockBroken`
override is gone, matching the three siblings already cleaned up above. The same body-matching also
settles a smaller review note: the guard's `OnBlockRemoved` match is anchored to the `Block`
overload's own signature (`OnBlockRemoved(IWorldAccessor, ...)`), so a file holding both a block and
its block entity can no longer satisfy it with the argument-less `BlockEntity` overload
`TeardownSymmetryTests` checks instead.

Twelve of the fourteen `OnStoreCollectibleMappings` bodies read
`stack?.Collectible.OnStoreCollectibleMappings(...)` - the `?.` guards the stack, not the collectible.
`ResolveBlockOrItem` can legitimately leave `Collectible` null (a mod removed, an item renamed)
without the stack itself being null, and these block entities keep the stack rather than scrubbing it
on load, so the bare `.Collectible.` access threw at `BlockSchematic.cs:426` on export - a silent
wrong answer turned into a crash. Fixed to `?.Collectible?.` at all twelve sites across eight files:
`BlockEntityMoltenCanalMoldPedestal`, `BlockEntityMoltenCanalTap`, `BlockEntityMoltenBarrel`,
`BlockEntityCastMold`, `BlockEntityRollingMill`, `BlockEntityHeatingHearth`,
`BlockEntityConverterControl`, `BlockEntityHopperBell`. `BlockEntityHopperTall` and
`BlockEntityHopperReinforced` were already safe - both null the stack in `FromTreeAttributes` whenever
its `Collectible` fails to resolve - and are left as they were rather than made uniform, since the
scrub there is already the better invariant of the two.

Also moved: `DesignTableDialogTests.cs`'s "internal (not private)" comment sat on the spy class
declaration and explained production's `CloseDialog` modifier two lines below; it now sits on the
`internal override` it actually describes.

`scripts/exmod.ps1 format` and `scripts/exmod.ps1 test all` - 15/15 targets green. All five suites
hold their counts from before this wave (`ExpandedLib.Tests` 1537, `IronworkingExpanded.Tests` 1571,
`LowPressureExpanded.Tests` 181, `SteelmakingExpanded.Tests` 243, `HighPressureExpanded.Tests` 54),
each still comfortably above its `test-floors.txt` floor; no floor change needed.

## 2026-08-09 - an overloaded engine labours; power raised; the cap was in the wrong units

Three rulings on top of the shaft-speed cap below.

**An overloaded engine no longer cuts out.** `PowerDemand` dropped to zero past twice the rated
load, which stopped the whole line dead - while a vanilla waterwheel merely bogs down and keeps
grinding, so the wheel read as the stronger machine even though it is not. Demand now stays at full
and the shaft simply crawls; it comes to a genuine stand only past four times the budget, where the
divisor clamp in `GetTorque` runs out of torque. That is a real limit instead of an arbitrary
multiple of a nominal rating. `IsMpOverstressed` survives to TELL the player the engine is labouring.

**`MpRatedLoad` follows the throttle.** It read `MaxPower`, which for the Cornish is the same 1.0 at
every setting, so the low setting delivered less power than a Watt while tolerating three times the
load - a throttled-back engine reading as the tougher one. It now reads `RunPower`. Nothing latches
off it any more, which is what made the constant safe to make live.

**`MpLoadPerEnginePower` 1.37 -> 2.0.** This is the right knob for "more powerful": top speed is
capped by `ShaftSpeed`, so raising this adds pulling power and load capacity without disturbing the
speeds the blower and pump are tuned around. A Watt now turns up to 2.4 load where a strong
waterwheel manages 1.5.

### The cap was in animation units, not network units

Caught by decompiling rather than by a test, and it made what shipped an hour earlier 1.59x too
permissive. `AnimationSpeed` is a CLIP-SPEED MULTIPLIER, not a network speed. The animator advances a
clip at `30 x AnimationSpeed` frames a second, so the 60-frame beam cycle strokes at
`0.5 x AnimationSpeed` cycles a second. The network turns its axle at `5 x speed` radians a second
(`ServerTick` hands `UpdateAngle` `speed * dt * 50`, and `UpdateAngle` adds `speed / 10`), that is
`5/2pi` revolutions a second, and `DriveMpCycleFrame` maps one revolution onto one beam cycle.
Equating them gives `speed = (pi/5) x AnimationSpeed`.

Using `0.5 + power` directly as the cap therefore let the shaft run `5/pi` faster than the engine
actually strokes - and under an MP generator the beam is phase-locked to the axle, so the engine's
own beam would have been driven 59% faster than its steam can work it. Exactly the violation the cap
was added to prevent, one level down. `ShaftSpeed` is now `CycleAnimationSpeed * (pi/5)`: Watt 0.503,
Cornish 0.440 / 0.565 / 0.817.

### Against ElectricalProgressive

Measured off `.compat/ElectricalProgressive`'s `egenerator`, whose params are
`[I_max, speed_max, resistance_factor, resistance_load, base_resistance, kpd_max]` and whose output
is `min(speed/speed_max, 1) x I_max` watts.

| generator | needs speed | Watt | Cornish low | Cornish normal | Cornish high |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| tier1, 128 W | 0.50 | **128 W** | 113 W | **128 W** | **128 W** |
| tier2, 256 W | 0.75 | 146 W | 101 W | 188 W | **256 W** |
| tier3, 512 W | 1.00 | 134 W | 91 W | 175 W | 328 W |

Tier 1 and 2 are comfortably served. Tier 3 wants shaft speed 1.0, which is **above any engine's
stroke rate**, so it cannot be maxed by adding engines: one Cornish high gives 328 W, two reach the
0.817 cap at 418 W, and a third adds nothing (it adds torque, not speed - the rule working as
intended). The old flat 1.5 cap let three engines brute-force 512 W, which is the 500 W figure
recorded in July.

That is a deliberate trade, and gearing is the answer the model already has: a 1.5x gear-up puts two
Cornish at the full 512 W, and vanilla's own large gear is 5.5x (which also works - the shaft drops
to 0.206 while the generator sees 1.131). Note the low end is BETTER than before: a single Cornish
high on tier 3 went 234 W -> 328 W with the power raise. Only the stack-three-engines route lost.



## 2026-08-09 - the shaft cannot outrun the engine turning it

Two rulings, one cause. A shaft coupled straight to an engine cannot physically spin faster than the
engine turns it, and players had chained engines onto a single helve hammer and got it hammering at
frame speed. Both come from the MP generator's top-speed cap being the CONSTANT `1.5 x MpRatedSpeed`
rather than the engine's own rotation rate: a lightly loaded line coasted to 1.5 whatever was driving
it, and stacking generators pushed it further still.

The engine already knew how fast it was turning - `AnimationSpeed = 0.5 + power` - it just was not
told to the network. That expression is now `BlockEntityEngine.ShaftSpeed` and both the beam
animation and the MP cap read it, so the flywheel the player watches can no longer amble while the
machines on the line race. Watt 0.8, Cornish 0.7 / 0.9 / 1.3, and a steam-starved engine genuinely
turns its line slower - `AvailablePower` carries the shortfall, and `PowerDemand` is binary (0 or 1)
so there is no circularity with `speed = budget / load`.

Chaining follows for free. Each generator stops pushing at ITS OWN engine's rate, so the line settles
at the fastest engine on it rather than the sum; the extra engines still add torque BELOW that speed,
which is load capacity - what a second engine is actually for. Simulated against the vanilla solver's
own integration:

| drive | old flat 1.5 cap | engine-speed cap |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Watt, one helve hammer | 1.364 | 0.739 |
| 8 Watts, one helve hammer | 1.472 | 0.780 |
| 1 Watt + 1 Cornish high | - | 1.258 (the fastest, not the sum) |
| 2 Watts, eight hammers | 0.41 -> 0.62 | 0.41 -> 0.62 (unchanged) |

Gearing is untouched and deliberately so: `updateNetwork` hands each node `speed * gearedRatio`, so
capping inside `GetTorque` bounds only the engine's own coupling and a geared-up branch may still run
faster - which is the one way a shaft is *supposed* to outrun its engine.

The taper had to be rescaled, not just repointed. It ran from rated speed to 1.5x; against a cap of
0.8 that formula divides by a negative. It now spans the top third of whatever the cap is, which is
the same proportion the old 1.0-to-1.5 band had. That proportion is load-bearing: sweeping it showed
a hard cut ripples 0.130 and a band of the top 20% ripples 0.062, while the top third settles dead
flat at both 2 and 6 nodes.

`MpGeneratorBehaviorTests` gains five tests, including one that runs the vanilla speed integration to
assert eight engines settle no faster than one. Mutation-checked by putting the 1.5 back: it fails
with the reported symptom in its message - one engine settles the line at 1.295, eight take it to
1.481.



## 2026-08-09 — throughput off the shaft, pressure off the torque: no more "% of rated"

Reported: an MP generator drove the blower at 1% of rated, or not at all. It is arithmetic. The
generator is a constant-power source, so the shaft settles at `speed = budget / load`; a Watt engine
at full output budgets `0.3 x 1.37 = 0.411` against the blower port's declared `0.8`, giving speed
`0.514`. The response band ran 0.5 to 1.5, so that is **1.4% of rated** - and any steam starvation
put the speed under 0.5, which was flat zero. The band's full-output point, 1.5, is exactly the
generator's *unloaded* top speed (`GetTorque` tapers to zero between rated and 1.5x rated), so
"100% of rated" required a shaft carrying no load at all - unreachable by a machine that is itself
the load. The whole usable range was squeezed into the bottom of the ramp.

So the rated-fraction model is gone rather than retuned. Both machines are positive displacement:

* **Throughput is a straight proportion of shaft speed.** `MpBlowerLitresPerSpeed` (40) and
  `MpPumpLitresPerSpeed` (5) replace the rated figure plus its two band edges. No floor, no
  ceiling - a shaft barely turning moves a trickle, which is what a bellows or a beam pump does.
  Speed 1.5 still gives the old 60 L/s, so the top of the range is unchanged; everything below it
  now scales instead of collapsing.
* **Pressure is carried by shaft load.** A displacement machine's torque draw is set by the pressure
  it works against, so the blower now tells its port `MpBlowerBaseLoad + MpBlowerLoadPerAtm x` the
  main's current pressure, and the pump's load carries its delivery head. That is the whole of the
  drive-size relationship: the network settles where the drive's torque meets the load, so a weak
  drive is dragged slower as the main fills and its output falls away, while a strong one holds
  speed to the ceiling. Nothing caps the pressure in code - the ceiling emerges from the shaft
  slowing, and `MpBlowerMaxPressure` stays only as the seal's own limit and the iron/steel tier gate.

`BEBehaviorMPFillerPort.SetLoad` lets a principal override its filler port's declared resistance;
`GetResistance` is read by the network solver every tick, so the principal writes it from its own
tick rather than the port going looking for the machine's state.

### Sized off the waterwheel, not the engine

The intended balance is that a waterwheel runs both machines slowly and steam runs them hard, and
that turned out to constrain the numbers far harder than the engine case did. A vanilla waterwheel is
not a constant-torque source: `BEBehaviorMPRotor.GetTorque` returns `(capableSpeed - speed) x
TorqueFactor`, so it seeks a speed and settles at `TargetSpeed - load/TorqueFactor`, with
`BEBehaviorMPWaterWheel` setting `TargetSpeed = min(0.3, flowRate)` and `TorqueFactor = flowRate`.

**A waterwheel cannot pass speed 0.3 even unloaded.** The old band's floor was 0.5, so a waterwheel
could never have driven either machine at any flow - the floor sat above the wheel's top speed. The
note that "a vanilla waterwheel turns at roughly speed 1" was simply wrong, and every figure derived
from it with it.

The drive spread is what sets the displacement: a well-fed wheel settles near 0.18 against these
loads, an engine's generator tapers out at 1.5, so steam turns the same machine some eight times
faster. Sizing the blower so a waterwheel just feeds one furnace at the blast gate therefore puts it
at 140 L/s per unit speed - a large-looking figure that is only the waterwheel's slow speed
multiplied back up. Loads came down accordingly, to 0.05 + 0.05/atm, or the wheel stalls outright.

That forces one inherited constraint to give: the mechanical pump cannot both be waterwheel-driveable
and stay under the engine pump's rate under steam, because the pump's allowed window is 5x wide and
the drive spread is 10x. The ordering test is now anchored where the machine is meant to be used - at
a waterwheel it beats the hand crank and loses to the engine pump. Under steam it beats the engine
pump, which is the point: that costs an engine, a generator and a line shaft, where the engine pump
bolts straight on.

Both drives are now pinned by tests that compute the settling speed from the vanilla solver's own
equations rather than quoting a speed.

### Numbers that came out of tests rather than judgement The Watt case is now asserted end to end from
the shipped constants, and it failed at first pass: 30 L/s per unit speed gave 16.4 L/s against a
24 L/s draw. The binding operating point is the furnace's own blast gate (1.5 atm) - below it the
furnace refuses the blast outright, above it the main is fuller than the furnace can draw down. That
first pass was still sized off the engine; the waterwheel anchor above then moved it again.
Separately, the load at the pressure ceiling has to stay under twice
an engine's rated load or the generator reads it as an overload and cuts the whole line out; at
`0.05 + 0.05 x 2.0 = 0.15` against a Watt's `0.822` it does, comfortably now that the loads have come
down for the waterwheel, and there is a test saying so.

Block info now reads the actual flow and the head, in all three languages - no percentage.


## 2026-08-09 — the wrap between a looping clip's last keyframe and its first is rendered

The stutter that survived the keyframe fix below. It is not easing, and it is not the loop
restarting: the animator's live frame space is `[0, quantityframes)`, so the stretch from the last
keyframe back to the first is an ordinary interpolation segment it draws like any other. At
`quantityframes` 60 with a last keyframe at 59, `PrevNextKeyFrameByFrame[59]` pairs frame 59 with
frame 0 and the blend length comes out as `60 - 59 + 0 = 1` - the entire return to the first
keyframe crammed into `[59, 60)`. Both cycles ended on a duplicate of frame 0, so inside that
segment every element authored zero at both ends sat at exactly its unposed default while `Axle`
(360 -> 0) and `Rod` (-360 -> 0) unwound a whole turn backwards. That is the snap to the unbuilt
pose, once per revolution, about one frame long.

`MPAnim.FrameFromAngle` spanned `total - 1`, so it never wrote into that segment itself - but
`RunningAnimation.Progress` runs after our write, one render stage later, and adds `30 * dt`. The
frame actually posed leads the frame written, and once a revolution the lead carried it across.

Fixed the way vanilla authors these, confirmed against `machine/jonas/pumphead1-complete.json`,
which is the identical case (60 frames, last keyframe 59): the turn ends one frame short at
`360 * 59/60 = 354` with `rotShortestDistance` set, and the code spans the whole frame count -
`BlockEntityMechPoweredBellows`, the only vanilla block that phase-locks a clip, writes
`AngleRad / 2pi * 60f`. Both `mpfluidpump` and `mpblower` now end their `Axle` at 354 and their
`Rod` at -354, flagged; `FrameFromAngle` spans `totalFrames`.

The art turns out to have been drawn on the 60-frame grid all along - the blower's keys sit at
0, 5 ... 55, six degrees apart, and `Rod`'s connecting-rod correction returns to exactly zero at
frame 55 only under that mapping. The old span played it slightly compressed.

Swept every shipped shape for the same defect and found one more: `engine/mpgenerator.json` runs its
`Axle` from -180 to +180 over frames 0, 30, 59, so its wrap unwound a full turn backwards too. Now
174 with `rotShortestDistanceZ`. The `[0, 15, 30, 45]` clips on cornish, watt, fluidpump and
airblower are fine - the wrap is an evenly spaced 15-frame return segment there - and the
single-keyframe `idle` clips just hold a pose.

`CycleAnimProgress` and `PhaseLockToEngine` carried the same off-by-one on either side of the
engine/sub-machine handshake; both now span the whole frame count.

`LoopingAnimationTests` holds the rule over every shipped shape, both halves of it: no element may
unwind half a turn or more across the wrap without `rotShortestDistance`, and none may be keyframed
at one end of a looping clip but not the other. Mutation-checked by putting the mpgenerator's 180
back - it names the file, clip, element and axis.

Raised `EaseInSpeed` from 1 to the vanilla default of 10 on both machines. The ease-in factor is
what the animator weights every pose by and it starts at zero on each `StartAnimation`, so at 1 the
machine was drawn part way toward its unposed shape for seconds after every start - and every
construction stage rebuilds the animator, which restarts it.

## 2026-08-09 — the judder at the end of the blower's cycle, and a shaft turning the wrong way

The blower's `cycle` keyframes `Piston1` and `Piston2` at frames 0, 15, 30 and 45 but not at 59,
while `Axle`, `Beam` and `Rod` all get an explicit last frame. The two tub pistons therefore held
their frame-45 value of +/-3 through the tail of the cycle and snapped home on the wrap - once per
revolution, which is exactly where the judder was. Both now carry a zero key at frame 59, matching
frame 0. That fixed the pistons but not the whole stutter - see the entry above for why the wrap is
not the no-op pose this assumed.

`MpBlowerTests` now asserts that the set of elements keyframed at frame 0 equals the set at the last
frame. A clip that is phase-locked and loops cannot afford an element that ends mid-stroke, and the
omission is invisible in the file - the animation still plays. Mutation-checked: dropping the two
new keys again fails it.

The pump's cycle was NOT short. A throwaway script said it had no frame-0 keyframe, which was the
script's own bug - PowerShell's `-not @(0)` is true, so the accumulator reset and swallowed every
leading zero. The file has always had a complete frame 0. Read the asset, not a summary of it.

Both machines' shafts turned against the axle driving them, plain to see next to a creative rotor.
The clips rotate their `Axle` element through +360 over the cycle, which runs the opposite way to
the vanilla axle for a rising angle. `MPAnim.LockFrameToAngle` takes a `reverse` flag rather than a
bare minus sign at the call sites, since which way a clip was authored is a property of the art and
worth naming.

## 2026-08-09 — the cycle animations now turn with the axle

The pump's shaft was dragging the wooden disc and the connecting rod round with it, because two
things were rotating the same elements at once: the shape's `cycle` clip keyframes `Axle` (with
`Gear` and `Rod` riding it as children), and the mechanical-power renderer was spinning that same
subtree on top. Restricting `GetShape()` to the axle only narrowed the overlap - it had to draw
*nothing*. The animator owns every moving part now; `BEBehaviorMpPumpDrive` joins the network and
reports its angle, and that is all.

Both machines' clips then had to follow the shaft rather than free-run. Each was starting `cycle`
at a hard-coded `AnimationSpeed = 1f` and never looking at the axle again, so the beam and pistons
kept their own time no matter how fast the shaft turned. `MPAnim.LockFrameToAngle` pins the running
clip's frame to the axle's render angle every render frame - a 250 ms tick shows as stepping - and
one revolution plays exactly one cycle at any speed. The blower reads its angle back through the
hosted `BEBehaviorMPFillerPort`, since its axle sits on a footprint cell rather than on the block.

`MPAnim` already existed here with **no callers at all**: the engine hand-rolled an equivalent
accumulator in `DriveMpCycleFrame` instead. That is the iwex fix worth having - the absolute mapping
cannot drift out of phase over a long run, needs no baseline reset when the clip restarts, and still
reverses for free, because the angle itself runs backwards. The engine now uses it too and its two
accumulator fields are gone. Nine tests cover the math, which had none while it had no callers.

Two things the write depends on, neither obvious, both documented at the helper: the clip must be
started with a non-zero `AnimationSpeed` (the animator does not pose a zero-speed animation, so
every frame written would be discarded), and it must loop, or the animator drops it and an
animator-rendered block loses its mesh.

Mechanical connections are no longer passthrough. Both machines took an axle on the drive face *or
its opposite*, so rotation ran straight out the far side; a machine's intake is the end of a line,
not a length of shafting. That tolerance is also what hid the drive-face contradiction fixed
earlier - two tests asserted east and west and neither failed.

Collection parallelism is off inside a test assembly (`test/xunit.runner.json`). The mods read
config live from a process-global register and several tests retune it mid-run, so a class
exercising production code could observe another class's temporary values. It surfaced as a 1.20
smex failure that appeared only under parallel load and passed every standalone re-run - dismissed
once as contention before it came back twice. Assemblies still run in parallel, which is where the
wall-clock actually comes from; a whole suite is a few hundred milliseconds. Six consecutive full
runs clean afterwards.

## 2026-08-09 — the art and the footprint were authored in opposite directions

With the footprint corrected the model was left half a turn out, still standing in the player. The
two were never one convention: the shape art runs along local -z while `fillerOffsets` are written
along local +z. So they meet in the middle - `shapebytype`'s rotateY is the plain placement angle,
`StructureAngle` carries the 180°, and rotating both by the same amount points the model and the
volume at each other. rotateY is back to north 0 / east 270 / south 180 / west 90, and both blocks
now say so where the angle is defined, since the natural assumption is that one rotation serves
everything.

The pump also arrived with its gear and shaft already fitted. The power renderer draws whatever
`GetShape()` returns and knows nothing about construction, so restricting it to the axle subtree
stopped it drawing the whole machine but still drew a shaft nobody had built. It now reads the
construction behaviour's own element selection and renders the subtree only once the stage that
installs it is present, seeded off `OnShapeChanged` so the shaft appears exactly when the stage is
built. "Nothing yet" has to be a selection that matches no element: an empty or null
SelectiveElements means no filter at all, which draws the entire machine.

## 2026-08-09 — port cells now take a plain right-click

The connector cells were plain fillers, and a plain filler swallows a placement click so nothing
can be built on the invisible footprint. That is correct for the body and wrong for a port: a cell
that exists to be attached to has to accept a pipe or an axle without sneak-placing, or a working
connector reads as a broken one. `allowAttach` already expresses exactly this - the boiler has used
it to open and close its own cells since it was written - so the four port cells now set it and no
code changed. The first attempt added a `PresentsPort` special case to `BlockStructureFiller`
instead; it was reverted, because the declarative flag already covers it and a second implicit rule
beside an explicit one is worse than either.

The pump's axle is the one case the flag cannot reach: it couples on the principal, not on a
filler, and a principal runs `ExRightClickConstructable`, whose interact handler sets PreventDefault
and returns handled on every click to drive its stages. `ExAttachIntent` declines the click there
when the held block is a connector and the machine is already finished - finished, because the same
wooden axle is one of the stacks the pump's own construction consumes, so while stages remain
building has to win. Nothing else needs it, and the blower override was removed once it was clear
both of its ports are fillers.

The pump's delivery also faced the wrong way. It leaves on local north, looking back down the body
into the notch of the L - the cell beyond the far end is outside the structure, while the notch cell
is the one a main can actually be run into.

Both machines now assert their port cells are attachable.

## 2026-08-09 — the two new machines were built in the wrong frame

Both placed their footprint on top of the player and presented every connector on the wrong face.
`BlockBoiler` has carried the answer since it was written: the body is authored along local +z, so
the placement angle is offset 180° to raise it AWAY from the player, and `shapebytype`'s rotateY is
offset to match. The blower and the pump took the shape offset but not the angle, so the visual and
the footprint disagreed by half a turn. Both now expose `StructureAngle` and route every offset and
facing through it.

That one frame accounts for all of it: the fillers land north of a north-facing block instead of on
the player, the blast main leaves on the north face, the pump delivers north, and the axle couples
on the west face of the blower's port cell and of the pump itself. A machine now delivers on the
same face it faces.

The pump's axle had a second fault underneath. `BlockMpFluidPump.DriveFace` derived the coupling
face independently of `BEBehaviorMpPumpDrive.SetOrientations`, and the two came out **opposite**.
`HasMechPowerConnectorAt` accepts a face or its opposite - both ends of one shaft line - so the
contradiction was invisible to every test, and two tests sat in the same file asserting east and
west without either failing. The behaviour now seeds from the block, and a test pins that they are
*equal*, not merely co-axial.

**Why the pump alone rendered finished.** `BEBehaviorMPBase.Initialize` sets `Shape = GetShape()`,
which defaults to `Block.Shape` - the whole machine - and hands the device to the mechanical-power
renderer through `AddDeviceForRender`. That renderer draws on its own, so the existing
`OnTesselation => false` override could never suppress it: the finished pump was painted over the
construction-staged mesh the block entity's animator draws. `GetShape()` now returns only the
rotating axle subtree, the same fix the converter transmission and engine generator already carry.
The blower was never affected - its port sits on a filler, whose shape is empty.

Footprint tests were resolving at a hard-coded angle 0, which is the local frame, so nothing ever
exercised a placed rotation. They now resolve at the block's own `StructureAngle`.

Block descriptions no longer state design decisions. "Blows a blast furnace without steam, but
never reaches converter pressure" is a balance ruling, not something a block tells you about
itself; same for the Watt engine being "the first step into steam power" and the Cornish being
"more effective". Replaced with what the machine does, in all three languages.

## 2026-08-09 — nine invisible bytes took the blast furnace recipes off the grid

From the first real play session's log. `smex:recipes/grid/blastfurnace.json` failed to
deserialize: *Invalid property identifier character: . Path '[0].ingredients.B.code', line 9*. The
character the message quotes is missing because it is `U+0001` - nine of them, each sitting where
eight spaces of indentation belong, in the three `refractorybrick-fired-*` ingredient blocks edited
for the any-tier-brick change. An editor shows nothing.

The file is a JSON *array*, so the parse failure discarded all six recipes in it: blast furnace
door, molten metal tap, tuyere, reinforced hopper and both bell hoppers. The blast furnace was
uncraftable and nothing said so outside the server log.

A repo-wide scan for control characters found one other file. `ExMeasure.cs` joins unit symbols on
a raw `U+0001` byte as a signature delimiter - deliberate and correct, but written as the byte
rather than as an escape. Now escaped, so it is visible to a reader and to a diff.

`ShippedJsonAssetTests` parses every JSON asset the three mods ship and rejects any control
character outside tab/LF/CR, naming the byte and offset. The build, the asset loader and 683 tests
were all green over a recipe file the game could not read; nothing in the suite had ever opened
one of these files as JSON.

Still open from the same log, and not yet root-caused: the MP blower and MP fluid pump render their
full shape at placement instead of building per construction stage, and a pipe will not connect to
the pump's delivery filler. Neither produces any log output. Ruled out so far: block and
block-entity classes both resolve (no registry errors among the 58 startup issues); shape element
names match the `addElements` paths exactly; the port network type, face convention, tree
persistence and offset rotation all agree with the boiler, whose port works.

The 156 migration warnings are `PipeMigration` mapping the three dropped `refractorytier*` bricks
onto `fire` twice over. The retained mapping is the intended one, so the outcome is right and the
noise is not.

## 2026-08-09 — the API patcher was killing the game before it could log

The client stopped starting: no window, no log file, and a console trace whose only frame was
`LoggerBase..cctor()` throwing `NullReferenceException` from inside `CrashReporter.Crash`. The
crash reporter needs the very type initializer that failed, so it could not report what it was
reporting — and no logger existed yet, so nothing reached disk. Not a mod fault: the whole mod
loader runs long after this.

`LoggerBase`'s static constructor throws a dummy exception at itself and reads
`new StackTrace(e, true).GetFrame(0).GetFileName().Split("VintagestoryApi")[0]` to learn where the
game was built. `GetFileName()` returns null when the runtime cannot find debug info for the
frame's assembly, and null is what it dereferences.

The debug info was missing because `scripts/tools/patch-api.cs` had rewritten
`.game/1.22/VintagestoryAPI.dll` through Mono.Cecil to publicize
`IPlayer.IsInInteractionRangeOf` (VS 1.22.6 made it `internal abstract`, which breaks every
`Substitute.For<IPlayer>()`). `asm.Write()` regenerates the assembly, and the regenerated file kept
only the `Reproducible` debug directory entry — the `CodeView` entry that points at
`VintagestoryAPI.pdb` was gone, so the shipped PDB could no longer be matched to it. Confirmed by
reading both debug directories: pristine 1.21 has `CodeView, PdbChecksum, Reproducible`, the
patched 1.22 had `Reproducible` alone. `.game/<slug>` is both the build's reference source and the
install the game launches from, so a test-only edit took the game down with it.

The patcher no longer rewrites anything. It resolves the MethodDef row through
`System.Reflection.Metadata`, computes the file offset of that row's Flags column, verifies the two
bytes there against the flags the metadata reader reported, and flips the access bits in place —
aborting rather than writing if the offset does not check out. The result differs from the shipped
assembly by exactly one byte (`0xc3` -> `0xc6` at offset 1398640), same length, `CodeView` intact,
PDB still matched. Both properties now hold at once: the member is public for the proxy generator,
and the game can still build a logger. Matching is by method name, not signature — an interface
proxy must implement *every* overload, and this one has two, of which only one was internal.

Also killed a hung `Vintagestory.dll` process left over from the failed launch: it never got past
logger init, never exited, and held the assembly open against replacement.

## 2026-08-09 — 0.9-support: mechanical power gets its own tier; the salvage rules invert

596 -> 683 green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22. exlib 0.7.0 -> 0.7.1, ppex 0.6.5 -> 0.6.6,
smex 0.9.5 -> 0.9.6 (config migrations ride the last two).

**The client crash in the exlib reports.** `ExContentGate.HideFromCreativeAndHandbook` set
`CreativeInventoryTabs` to null. The asset loader never leaves it null, and game code relies on
that: `AttachableInteractionHelp.GetOrCreateInteractionHelp` walks every collectible and reads
`.Length` on it with no guard while building the help for an attachable entity — so the client
died the moment a player looked at a boat, a raft or a mount. Decompiled to confirm; hiding is
expressed by *length* (`GetHandBookStacks` tests `Length != 0` and does guard), so the array is
now emptied instead. Two reporters found it by disabling molds, but the crushed-coke retirement
shipped in 0.9.5 calls the same helper unconditionally — it would have hit everyone. The stacks
list stays null on purpose: that is the loader's own default and the code path that handles it.

The other report — `Could not load type 'ExpandedLib.EntityRegistry.EntityRegisterAttribute'` —
is an old ppex/smex built against exlib 0.6.x loading against 0.7.0, possible only because those
releases pinned the placeholder `exlib@0.1.0`. Real pins shipped in the last batch, so it cannot
recur going forward; it cannot be fixed retroactively for pairs already released.

**Two machines that run on an axle instead of steam.** Both are right-click constructed, and the
grid recipe buys only the wooden frame.

- `smex:mpblower` — the twin-tub bellows, the first air source that needs no boiler. 60 L/s at
  full axle speed against a 2.0 atm ceiling. That ceiling is the whole design: it clears the
  furnace's new 1.5 atm gate and never reaches the converter's 2.5, so mechanical power makes
  iron and steam is still what makes steel. Nothing branches on tier — the ordering *is* the gate,
  and it is asserted as an ordering. A vanilla waterwheel turns at about speed 1, so half rated
  output, 30 L/s against the two tuyeres' 24 L/s draw.
- `ppex:mpfluidpump` — the walking-beam pump, which fills a boiler whose fire is out. 8 L/s at a
  fixed 1.5 atm head; between the hand crank and the engine pump by design, pinned as an ordering.
  Both ports are on fillers at the far end where the shape draws its valve column: source under
  the low cell, delivery out the face of the high one.

**exlib: footprint cells can host block-entity behaviours.** A `fillerOffsets` entry takes
`behaviors: [{ code, face, properties }]`; the filler instantiates each and hands it the connector
face already rotated into the placed orientation. Mechanical-power discovery is position-local — a
principal three cells away cannot accept an axle at the cell the axle touches — so the presence has
to live on the cell. `BEBehaviorMPFillerPort` is the minimal node for it, and `BlockStructureFiller`
became an `IMechanicalPowerBlock` that answers only for cells actually hosting one. Ported from the
ironworking branch with its two client-sync fixes intact: a behaviour created after `FromTree` gets
the tree replayed (client only — the server discovers its own network), and a set that arrives as a
sync update rather than at `Initialize` is created from `FromTreeAttributes`.

**The furnace's blast gate is its own.** `BlastPressureThreshold` was read by both the furnace and
the converter, so lowering one lowered the other. Split: `BfBlastPressureThreshold` 1.5 for the
furnace, the old key stays 2.5 and now means the converter only.

**Charcoal in the burden.** The bell hopper counts one shared carbon unit rather than two quotas,
so a batch takes coke, charcoal or any mix. Scaling by *both* configured amounts keeps the
arithmetic exact in integers whatever they are set to: coke is worth `HopperCharcoalRequired`,
charcoal `HopperCokeRequired`, a batch costs the product. Coke is spent first.

**Refractory tiers relaxed.** Every refractory structure takes any brick tier; only the converter
still pins one, at tier2. The heat sink is rebuilt in refractory brick rather than iron sheet —
what a regenerator is actually made of — with a tier variantgroup and a migration rewriting placed
ones to tier3.

**Breaking a machine stopped being a punishment.** The boiler dropped only its construction
materials, never the crafted frame it was placed from, so taking one down to move it was a net
loss; boilers and engines now hand the frame back. The converter is the one exception — it is
spawned by its control block and has no item to return — so it returns the placement cost instead,
the large gear and the rods. Salvage went 0.8 -> 1.0 in both mods: the tax only discouraged
rebuilding, and a *burst* boiler is a separate path that keeps its 0.4. Every `requiredMiningTier`
is gone; they gated breaking on a pickaxe a player who could build the machine already had.

Also: the quad-rod mold asks 400 units, since each rod is 100.

**Formatting follows the ironworking branch.** `.editorconfig` came across and `scripts/format.*`
runs the two passes it describes — CSharpier for the wrapping, then `dotnet format` for the
same-line braces CSharpier cannot emit. Order matters; reversed, the Allman braces survive.

Tests: 87 added. The filler-hosting suite ported from the other branch (parse, rotate, serialize,
host, the MP glue, the port itself); the blower's gate ordering, air budget and footprint; the
pump's rate placement, drive face and port layout; the carbon arithmetic across single-fuel, mixed
and short feeds. The charcoal tests were mutation-checked — `CarbonPerCharcoal` mis-set, four tests
red, file restored and verified by hash. Eleven existing tests pinned the *old* drop and mining
rules exactly as they should have; they were rewritten to the new ones rather than deleted.

Documentation swept against the code: both new machines written up, the handbook corrected where it
quoted the wrong lid vent rate, steam temperature range, Cornish construction cost and firebox door
name, and the wiki's claim that exlib is bundled into ppex.dll removed. ru and uk brought level with
en across all three mods, including the passages that still described crushed coke.

## 2026-08-09 — 0.9-support: coke goes into the burden whole; the settled balance calls

596 -> 599 green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22. Versions bumped for the config migrations to fire:
ppex 0.6.4 -> 0.6.5, smex 0.9.4 -> 0.9.5 (and smex's ppex pin with it).

- **Crushed coke is retired; the furnace takes coke.** The intermediate existed only to be fed to
  the blast furnace, and producing it meant bolting a `crushingProps` entry onto vanilla coke -
  which put smex in the middle of every other mod's crushing economy, and was the root of the
  Expanded Matter complaint rather than the patch that was reported. That patch and the
  config gate written for it are both gone. `HopperCokeRequired` is restated in whole coke:
  3 crushed was 1.5 coke at the old 1:2 crush ratio, so 2. Matches how iwex already works.
  - The item is deliberately still *declared*. An item code that stops resolving takes every
    existing stack with it, so it has to outlive the world it is being migrated out of. It is
    hidden from creative and the handbook instead, and has no source and no use.
  - `CrushedCokeMigration` converts the stacks one-for-one as chunks load and as players join. One
    for one rather than the 1:2 crush ratio: it hands a player slightly more fuel value than they
    had, which is the safe direction for a migration nobody opted into.
- **exlib migrations gained an item table.** The remap machinery was block-only - `GetBlock` gated
  entry, so an item code could never even enter it - and `RemapInventory` matched every stack in
  one table keyed by code. Since an item and a block may legitimately share a code, that could
  rewrite an item into a block, or delete it outright as an unresolvable purge. Stacks now route by
  `EnumItemClass` through separate tables, and `IItemCodeMigration` is the item-side declaration.
- **The MP overstress ceiling scales with the shaft.** It judged a shared network's whole
  resistance against ONE engine's rating, so adding engines could not raise it: a bank stalled well
  below what it could really drive, and re-engaged unpredictably. `DrivenRatedLoad` sums the rating
  of every engine on the shaft, and the re-engage point sits at 1.6x against the 2x cut-out so a
  load parked on the threshold cannot chatter. No behaviour change for a single-engine shaft.
  With that in place `MpLoadPerEnginePower` went 0.875 -> 1.37, which is what actually lands three
  Cornish engines near the 500 W players were measuring against an electrical generator.
- **Blast furnace exhaust pushes at 2 atm** (`BfExhaustOutputPressure`), where it was an implicit
  1.0. The relief valve the setup diagrams show defaults to a 1 atm gate, so against a 1-atm-capped
  producer it could never open - the documented relief path was decorative. Burst is 5 atm on iron
  and 10 on steel. A run with any open end is still clamped back to 1 atm.
- **The reinforced hopper syncs on every inventory mutation**, not only the bell's drain, so a
  vanilla chute feeding it can no longer leave a stale client copy either.

Player-facing text follows the coke change across en.json, both handbook articles, the moddb page
and the smex README - including the passage that taught the *old* choke behaviour ("a choked or
backfed exhaust ... will snuff it out"), which is now wrong: a blocked flue stalls the furnace and
it recovers. **ru.json and uk.json still describe crushed coke** in `advancedsteelmaking-text` and
`handbook-blastfurnace-text`; those are hand translations and are left for the translator.

## 2026-08-09 — 0.9-support: second fix batch, from the player reports

585 -> 596 green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22. Every fix mutation-checked: reverted, tests confirmed red,
restored.

- **Engine stroke sounds no longer fire on a backward step.** `PistonCycleSounds.Crossed` read
  `cur < last` as "the animation wrapped past the end", but a cycle running backwards - a reversed
  mechanical network, or the sub-machine phase-lock writing the frame directly - arrives the same
  way. On a backward step the wrap branch is true for every keyframe *except* the tiny interval
  actually stepped over, so on the 50 ms client tick a single engine emitted roughly twenty plays a
  second per keyframe instead of two a revolution. A handful of machines then exhausted the game's
  250 concurrent-sound cap, at which point *all* audio starts getting dropped, vanilla's included.
  The predicate now measures the forward distance travelled on the loop and refuses a delta larger
  than half a revolution, which no real tick covers.
- **Machine loops are typed as ambience.** `SoundParams.SoundType` has no initialiser, so every mod
  sound was `EnumSoundType.Sound` - an effect. That is why they never appeared in accessibility
  mods' sound lists and could not be muted there.
- **The bell hopper syncs the hopper it drains.** It called `MarkDirty` on itself after consuming
  from the reinforced hopper above, but `MarkDirty` is per position and the client reads those eight
  slots from the *hopper's* block entity tree, so only the magazine was ever pushed. `slot.MarkDirty`
  reaches a client only while that client has the dialog open, which is exactly why the contents
  looked right until the dialog was reopened, and why relogging cleared it.
- **Light absorption on four non-cube blocks.** The three slag path variants and the cowper heat
  sink declared 99, the value vanilla reserves for full opaque cubes (rammed earth, daub, diamond);
  vanilla's own stone paths do not set it at all. They rendered near-black under a lighting mod.
  Paths now match vanilla at 0, the heat sink keeps a token 1. The blast furnace tap and tuyere sit
  at 3 and were left alone - mild, and not the reported blackness.
- **EM's coke crushing is config-gated instead of patched out.** smex shipped a JSON patch pinning
  `/2/enabled` to false in EM's crushing recipes - unconditional, and positional: it disabled
  whatever recipe happened to sit at index 2. All three of EM's recipes output `em:crushed-ore-coal`
  (from charcoal, from coal ore, from coke), so only the ingredient distinguishes them. Replaced
  with `EmCokeCrushingGate`, which matches on the coke ingredient and honours
  `enableEmCokeCrushing` (off by default: one coke into four crushed coal is worth about double the
  fuel duration the coke itself carries, which undercuts the chain the mod is built around). A
  player who wants EM's crafting route as the bridge between the two mods can now switch it on.

Reverted from the previous batch: the `x3` in the two engine sub-machines is playtest calibration,
not a stray coefficient - see the entry below.

## 2026-08-09 — 0.9-support: fixes translated back from the ironmaking branch

`0.9-support` branched off main at 28e9f7c to carry a fixes release for the public line while the
ironmaking rewrite matures. Nothing cherry-picks between them — the rewrite renamed ppex to lpex,
extracted hpex and moved the iron chain into iwex — so each fix below was re-derived against the 0.9
shapes and tested here. Baseline before the work: 118 / 99 / 119 / 235 = 571 green on 1.20, 1.21 and
1.22; 576 after.

- **Production and network ticks clamp their dt.** `BlockEntityProductionMachine.RunProductionTick`
  and `BlockNetworkModSystem.OnServerTick` both forwarded the engine dt verbatim, and every grace
  timer downstream does `Elapsed += dt` against a threshold with no per-step bound. A server tick
  longer than the grace window therefore crossed it in one call: the boiler's 30 s over-pressure
  grace fires `Explode()`, the engine's 60 s fires `Break()`, the pipe run's 30 s bursts a segment.
  The boiler is the worst case because the same oversized dt also drives it into over-pressure —
  `BoilStep` flashes `SteamPerSecond * dt / 16` litres with no overshoot cap, which at dt=60 is the
  whole water charge. Both sites now cap at twice their own interval. The two listeners have
  different exposure: a block entity's is re-stamped on chunk load, but the network listener is
  registered once at startup and never unregistered, so it sees every stall for the whole uptime.
- **Multiblock structure angle is established in `Initialize`.** `_currentAngle` starts at -1, is not
  persisted, and was only ever assigned by the 3000 ms monitor tick — but a structure that loads
  already complete registers its 1000 ms production tick in `Initialize`, so it took three ticks at
  -1. `ExOrientation.RotateOffset` normalises -1 to 359 and falls through its `_ => (x, z)` default,
  silently reading as unrotated, so every structure-local offset resolved to the wrong block: a
  rotated blast furnace scanned the wrong hearth cell and cached tuyeres and gas outlets at unrotated
  coordinates. The converter control already carried this fix locally; it is now in the base class
  for every machine. Left the converter's own call in place — the base fix is server-side and the
  converter's runs on both sides, where the client needs the angle to render.
- **Molten canal levelling moves half the gap, not all of it.** `MoltenNetwork.FlowEdge` moved the
  whole difference, so an adjacent pair inverted every tick — 40/0 became 0/40 and back — and a run
  sloshed instead of settling. Drain fittings (pedestal, tap) still take everything offered so a run
  can empty. The minimum-flow floor now gates on the gap rather than on the halved step, or a gap
  between one and two minimums would sit permanently out of reach.
- **Orientation relaxation actually relaxes.** `BlockNetworkNode.ComputeValidOrientations` passed
  single orientation letters to `BlockFacing.FromCode`, which wants the full word and returns null
  for every letter — so the fallback removed nothing and recomputed an identically empty result.
  `RecalculateAndSyncOrientations` reads that empty result as unsupported and calls `BreakBlock`, so
  a bend with a connector against a solid wall and a network neighbour on a third face popped out of
  the world on a neighbour change. Now uses the repo's own `SideToFace` map.
- **Smoke-stack masonry legend accepts clinker brick.** The legend enumerated seven of vanilla's
  eight `brickcourse` colours and omitted clinker, and its `claybricks-good-fire` literal could not
  match `claybricks-clinkerrough`; both are real placeable blocks, so a chimney built from them never
  completed and the only feedback was the outline. Replaced the colour enumeration with
  `brickcourse-.*` — the enumeration is exactly what let one go missing. Kept `.*` rather than `*`:
  the string sits inside `@()` and is read as a regex, where `brickcourse-*` is a quantifier on the
  dash.

Then, against the player reports:

- **A blocked flue stalls the blast furnace instead of extinguishing it.** Two independent reports
  of a furnace dying on a plant built to the documented schematic. Shutting the hot exhaust off to
  a cowper stove is exactly what the handbook's regenerator swap requires, but `IsChoked` counted as
  a disruption, so 30 s later `Extinguish()` dumped the molten iron, slagged every hearth pile and
  ended a twenty-minute campaign. The choke now gates the heat target and the melt instead: the
  furnace falls back to its natural draught ceiling, which sits below iron's melting point, so the
  existing below-melting demotion walks it back to Firing and it resumes when the exhaust reopens.
  Also fixed the choke test itself - it read "any outlet refused" where it meant "no outlet
  accepted", so piping one gas outlet and leaving the other bare choked the furnace permanently,
  and the HUD line that names the reason sat in the Idle branch where `IsChoked` is never set, so
  the player got no explanation at all.
- **The `x3` in both engine sub-machines is named, not removed.** It reads as a stray coefficient
  against the config comments (which quote 5 L/s for a Watt-driven pump where rate x power is 15),
  and was briefly taken for one. It is not: it is playtest calibration, and the quoted figures are
  the *realised* rates once the intake draw and the output main's free capacity bite. Dropping it
  makes the pump and blower three times weaker than they play. It is now a documented
  `ThroughputScale` constant on each sub-machine saying exactly that, so the next reader does not
  repeat the mistake. Both rates are now pinned by tests, which is what was actually missing - no
  test asserted any pump or blower rate as a number, so either term could have changed unnoticed.
- **A config that cannot be read is no longer silently replaced.** `ExConfigRegister.Load` ended in
  an unconditional write, so every path that failed to reproduce the player's values overwrote
  their file with defaults - and an empty or whitespace-only file deserializes to null rather than
  throwing, so the most destructive case logged nothing at all. Unreadable files are now copied to
  `<name>.corrupt` first. The intermittency in the report is the second half: singleplayer runs a
  client and a server ModLoader against the *same* ModConfig folder, so two writers raced over one
  file. Only the server writes now, which is the rule the recipe-catalogue registry already
  followed. The class doc claiming "safe on either side; each side reads its own local copy" was
  the false premise the bug rested on, and is corrected.
- **Dependency pins name real versions.** smex pinned `ppex@0.1.0` and `exlib@0.1.0`, placeholders
  that no release ever carried, which is why a player whose ppex failed to load saw
  `ppex@0.1.0 - Missing` and reasonably read it as smex demanding a version that does not exist.
  The packaging itself is sound - all nine release zips were opened and verified, and the game's
  own `SemVer` comparators were executed against both directions of the legacy/current split. The
  Cake packager now fails the build if a legacy target's game dependency was not actually rewritten,
  which today silently no-ops when a source modinfo spells its game version any other way.

Tests: 571 -> 585, green on 1.20, 1.21 and 1.22. The two clamp cases plus a control that an ordinary
dt passes through unchanged; exact-split and settles-over-20-ticks cases for canal levelling; exact
throughput for the pump and the blower; corrupt/blank/absent config cases plus both sides of the
write rule; and blocked-flue cases asserting the extinguish timer never starts and the charge is
untouched, with an open-flue control. Tightened three assertions that could not fail:
`A_tick_flows_metal_across_the_connector_and_conserves_the_total` (asserted only that the neighbour
got *something*, and passed identically when the pair swapped) and the pump/blower "did it move"
checks. Every fix above was mutation-checked - the fix reverted, the new tests confirmed red, the
fix restored. Two fixtures needed correcting to make that possible: the config store tests never
declared a side (the substitute default is Client, under which nothing is written), and the blast
furnace rig had no gas outlets at all, so the entire choke path was unreachable.

truth only. The two audit files were rebuilt as open-findings documents (80 → 46 and 90+22 → 38+9,
everything overtaken by execution deleted). The expansion plan shrank 3773 → 2299 lines: executed
unit bodies (U2, U3, U4.1–4.3, U5) stubbed to one-line records, facts pending units relied on
relocated first. STATE.md rewrote down to ~260 lines of verified-current blockers and settled

## 2026-08-07 — Test-tree reorganization: casting merge, parity tests out of Fixtures/, pipe tests to iwex, seeds out of the harness

Build clean; green on 1.21: exlib 871 · iwex 1568 (+13) · lpex 180 (−13) · hpex 54 · smex 243 — total

to iwex because they touch no lpex type. Floors untouched (lpex 180 ≥ 176, iwex 1568 ≥ 1550).
